Each side erases `prov_env` at `prov_erase_minute = min(pair-open + 45 d, prov_anchor_minute + 119 d)`; a responder that never sealed and an initiator never acknowledged keep it until the session is deleted (no provisional envelope of that session exists yet; an unacknowledged initiator session is demoted at `UNACK_MAX` and retired 46 days later).
5. **Derivation (no loss inside CS-3).** A responder R seals provisional only before `anchor_R + 28 d`; the initiator I's anchor is its first decryption of an R message, which happens after R's first provisional seal, so R's provisional envelopes are authored before `anchor_I + 28 d` and accepted until `+ FRESH + 2σ`: I needs `anchor_I + 79 d`. I seals provisional (only when R's root has not reached it) before `anchor_I + 28 d`, and under CS-3's premise I consumed R's first reply within its relay lifetime (≤ 37 d 2 h, §7.6), so `anchor_I ≤ anchor_R + 37 d 2 h`; R needs `anchor_R + 37 d 2 h + 28 d + 45 d + 6 d = anchor_R + 116 d 2 h`, rounded up to 17 weeks (119 d) for both roles.
6. **P-7 and L7-D21 restated.** A provisional envelope is unattributable once both devices passed their `prov_erase_minute` (each at most `prov_anchor_minute + 119 d`, earlier through the pair-open rule) and the recipient of the session's first flight erased that generation (≤ 91 d after supersession, §5.4).
7. **Declared (L7-D6 extended).** A side that has sealed provisional for 28 days without receiving its peer's root re-establishes at its next user send (a bundle fetch linked to a deposit).
8. **Tests and mutants.** SCN-G gains a FAILED default-timer retry across a week boundary and a retry after the peer's `prov_erase_minute`: no bundle fetch, no PreKey message, displayed once. The T34 world gains a one-reply conversation that asserts the 119-day bound on both devices; EM-2 checks rules 3–5. Mutant **MC-33** (a retry without the kept out key) is detected by the SCN-G week-boundary variant; mutant **PM-39** (`prov_env` kept past `prov_anchor_minute + 119 d`) by the T34 one-reply variant.

#### 22.8.6 PQ-C offers follow the peer's live generations (CRY-6)

**Defect.** After a peer's restore, reset or renewal, an unacknowledged `seal_root_pq` was re-attached with identical bytes, and a new offer encapsulated to the newest `dm_peer_pq` key, which was still the pre-restore in-band key: the peer could never derive the root, so pair mode from the peer stayed blocked for up to `start(b + 1) + 45 d`, driving provisional expiry and re-establishment churn. R11 had no rule for an unopenable `seal_root_pq`, and §5.4's retention derivation ignored re-attachment after the target's use window.

**Rules.**
1. **Renewal and key decision.** A renewal from c (R-SS1 making a new session `current`) and a KEYDEC acceptance delete c's `dm_peer_pq` rows of source `in_band`, and every `bundle` row not named by the `peer_gen_id` of c's `current` session; they discard c's unacknowledged PQ offers (`root_env` and `offer_env` to NULL; the generation counter is kept) and insert a classical `seal_root` offer. A PQ offer is used again only after a `pq_key` whose `bundle_week` is at least the week of the renewal has arrived.
2. **Re-attachment window.** A stored `seal_root_pq` is re-attached only while its target key is usable (`now < start(bundle_week + 5)`); afterwards a new offer replaces it (PQ to a newer key, else classical). §5.4 step 3 then holds for re-attached offers too.
3. **R11.** A `seal_root_pq` that does not open (`NOT_OPEN`, an unknown `peer_gen_id`) is ignored and counted (`pq_root_unopenable`); the rest of the L0 applies.
4. **Test.** PQ6: a restored and a reset peer with a stored PQ offer and an old in-band key: pair mode from the peer resumes within one round trip after its next `pq_key` or classical root, with no `NO_SEAL_KEY`.

#### 22.8.7 Card holders: fetch credential, polling, inbox reads with a token, the sender's card, credential wording, PQ-B provenance (PH-1, PH-3, PH-5, PH-13, CRY-10)

**Defects.** (1) `GetBlobResponse` returns `expiry_unix_seconds` beside `uploaded_at` (`protocol/relay/v1/relay.proto:45-49`), filled from the stored expiry (`relay/crates/node/src/lib.rs:355-359`), which is `ceil_hour(store + ttl)` (`relay/crates/storage/src/lib.rs:22-24,59-65`); bundles use a fixed 30-day TTL, so `expiry − 30 d` gives every card holder the upload hour, and `list` with the free shared read key can be polled to see the minute a bundle appears. §1.2, D8, §11.2, L7-D10 and §21.9 point 4 said "never an hour". (2) A write capability also grants read (`relay/crates/capability/src/lib.rs:119-121`), so a card holder with one token lists and gets my inbox, opens the outer layer's recognition with `KIO` and learns, alone, the minute of every intro arrival, its `gen_id`, and which conversations I have not answered; only a colluding card holder was declared. (3) A first message gives the recipient the sender's full card (§6.4 Q), irreversibly, including recipients who never accept and honeypot cards; undeclared. (4) §9.6 called credentials hashes; they carry the secret. (5) PQ-B said card-derived keys are never transported by GHOST, but every intro inner layer carries the sender's card.

**Rules.**
1. **FETCH credential kind (replaces the reviewer's "coarsen every v3 read").** `KeyKind` gains `KEY_KIND_FETCH = 4`; credential v3 kind `0x04`; verifier label `"ghost/v1/fetch-verifier"`; rights `list` and `get` only. The owner registers the bundle namespace's shared secret `RP_{i,s}` (renamed the **fetch secret**) as FETCH; card holders fetch with it. For `get` authorized by FETCH, relays return `uploaded_at` floored to the start of its ISO week and `expiry_unix_seconds` rounded **up** to the next ISO week boundary (still ≤ now + 30 d + 1 h + 7 d, inside `relay_client.rs`'s bound; tombstones only grow). READ (the owner's inbox key `RX`) keeps §21.9 point 4's floored `uploaded_at` and the exact hour expiry, so C-11 and ADR-27 C.9 are unchanged. No new information reaches relays: a bundle namespace was already distinguishable (it never has a DEPOSIT registration). `key_kind` gains `fetch` (B.5); `register.txt`, the T21 matrix (fetch at store, check and on an inbox namespace refused), and a `relay_semantics.txt` case follow. Mutant **PM-38** (exact expiry on a FETCH get) is detected by NI-DM6's AD-7 variant, which now compares every field of every get response.
2. **Polling, declared.** A card holder that lists `P` on its own schedule learns the minute each bundle first appears: the owner's first time-triggered pass after `t_pub` each week (hence absences) and the activation and restore minutes. §1.2, D8, §11.2 and L7-D10 say so; the NI-DM6 AD-7 variant reports a polling list view (not gated, since it is declared). A relay-side visibility floor is Q69.
3. **Inbox reads with a card holder's own token, declared.** L7-D3, L7-D16, §11.2, §11.4 and Q36 now state that a card holder **alone** with one token per relay sees intro-envelope arrivals to the minute, their `gen_id` and X-Wing ciphertext, and which conversations remain unanswered (their envelopes stay intro), plus inbound volume and timing. T32 gains an AD-7-with-token attacker (reported with ground truth). Q36 is re-asked with a concrete owner-bound read rule.
4. **The sender's card, declared (L7-D25, Phase 13 copy).** Sending a first message, request or reconnect hands the recipient my full card for good: it can deposit, fetch my bundle, list my inbox with its own token, recognise my intro envelopes and, colluding with relays, tie my inbox and bundle namespaces to my `ghost1` identity, until card rotation (Phase 13). The Phase 13 UI states this before the first send to a card that arrived as a link or a request. A reduced reply card is Q70.
5. **§9.6 credentials row** (in place): bearer secrets seen by the serving relay and, under PQ-R4, by a recording quantum adversary; they grant deposit, read or fetch at that relay slot while registrations live, never opening keys; only the stored verifiers are hashes.
6. **PQ-B provenance (CRY-10).** PQ-B case 1 reads "derived from a card that reached the device in person, through an out-of-band link (PQ-R8), or inside an X-Wing-keyed intro inner layer"; T35's CARD tag carries its source {IN_PERSON, LINK, TRANSPORTED(KEM)}, and the T35 report lists LINK-sourced sealing as the PQ-R8 exception.

#### 22.8.8 Claims restated: AD-1 and AD-V, "100% privacy", verifiability, native symbols (PH-2, PH-4, PH-8, PH-9, PH-11)

1. **§11.4 and §11.6** (in place): relays see establishment, reset, reconnect and stale re-establishment as bundle fetches linked to a deposit (L7-D6), and activation and restore moments (L7-D10); envelope bytes and sizes hide type, first contact and key change (P-4); relay-stored bytes are PQ-sealed except the card-keyed layers when a card travelled over a classical channel (PQ-R8).
2. **AD-1 and AD-V.** With closed-source distribution the operator of AD-1 ("poate publica versiuni noi", TM:49) and the vendor of AD-V are in practice one party. P-1 … P-8 and every "Operatorul nu poate …" statement hold only for the logged, auditor-reproduced build (§1.3, ADR-38, B.3). The plan's example wording (plan:373) is amended in B.1: it may appear only next to the L-SRC residue and the attestations it relies on, and not before the ADR-38 release gates exist.
3. **Enforcement gaps of the transparency log (ADR-38 point 6).** The updater's inclusion check does not cover a first install, updates delivered by a store client (Accrescent, an F-Droid-format repository), or relay and issuer binaries run by independent operators. Rules: a standalone verifier (outside the app, published with the specification) checks an APK's log inclusion before a first install; relay and issuer releases are logged and attested (T12b); operators check relay binaries against the log and the attestations before running them; operator independence (ADR-11) is declared to give no protection against AD-V. The AD-V residue (B.3), L7-D23 and ADR-38's consequences say so; the "(+)" sentence is rewritten; Q71 asks whether store channels without the log check stay.
4. **Verifiability wording (ADR-38 consequences, plan:202 erratum).** The specification, vectors and models are checkable by anyone; the shipped binary's behaviour is attested only by named auditors and the log. The black-box kit's scope is stated item by item (onion-only traffic and bucket sizes as seen through the user's own exported card and a published relay client; manifest and native-library checks), with what it cannot check: P-1 … P-8 and T1 at relays.
5. **Native symbols (PH-11).** The DM cryptography ships as stripped native code (`ghost/Cargo.toml:87-89`), which makes "closed, not opaque" weak for exactly the privacy-critical code. Each release publishes the native symbol files of `libghost_client_net.so`, hash-bound to the shipped library, beside its log entry (ADR-38 point 9; Q61); until the first release the residue is declared in L7-D23.

#### 22.8.9 Repository, licence and CI (IMPL-01, IMPL-02, IMPL-09, PH-10 = IMPL-15)

1. **Public horizon.** Everything pushed before the visibility switch is public: at least `b2df181` (`origin/main`, pushed 2026-09-16T10:22:35Z) and the CI artifacts listed then (299 on 2026-09-16, including `ghost-client-net-jniLibs` of 15 600 339 B for `b2df181`, created 2026-09-16T11:01:59Z; 90-day retention). The owner records the last public SHA and the artifact inventory at the switch; counsel's review (R-39, ADR-37 point 10) covers that set.
2. **Owner actions are preconditions of every code slice, S1–S3 included, in this order:** (a) delete the public artifacts and disable every APK, AAR and `.so` upload **now** (they still run); (b) switch the repository to private; (c) proprietary `LICENSE`, Cargo `license-file` replacing `license = "MIT OR Apache-2.0"`, `[licenses.private] ignore = true`. §16.4, §17.1, §17.2 item 13 and ADR-37/ADR-39 agree: the licence change is a pre-code step, the notices and licence screen are the pre-distribution step.
3. **Native hand-off without public binaries (IMPL-01).** Today `android` and `reproducible` receive the library only through the `ghost-client-net-jniLibs` artifact (`ci.yml:160-179,262-270`). While the repository is public, `android`, `reproducible` and `dm-emulator` each build the native libraries themselves at the same fixed path, and `android-native` publishes its sums as a job output (text), which every consumer compares with its own build. Once the repository is private, internal hand-off artifacts are allowed with `retention-days: 1`; G-L5 gains a fixture that allows exactly those hand-offs and only in a private repository.
4. **CI plan (IMPL-09).** Private repositories run on 2-CPU, 8 GB standard Linux runners (public ones: 4 CPU, 16 GB), and GitHub Free includes 2 000 minutes per month and 500 MB of artifact storage, with usage blocked at the quota without a payment method (GitHub documentation, fetched 2026-09-16); the USD 200 estimate came from public 4-CPU runners. S0′ re-derives the plan on the private runner class: measured time per job, `timeout-minutes`, shard counts, monthly minutes including the Phase 9 jobs, `retention-days` (1–7) on every upload and a storage budget or payment method. Triggers follow ADR-39: `messaging-exit-gate`, `dm-privacy-gate` and `dm-emulator` run nightly and on dispatch; pull requests run the small variants in `android`. Q62 is amended.

#### 22.8.10 Gates, pins and dependency facts (IMPL-03 … IMPL-08, IMPL-10 … IMPL-14, IMPL-16, IMPL-17, PH-12 = IMPL-06, CRY-8, CRY-9)

1. **cargo-deny bans (IMPL-03).** `bans.deny` entries are package specs, not globs: with cargo-deny 0.20.2, `{ crate = "libcrux-*" }` over a graph holding 14 `libcrux-*` crates gives "bans ok", `{ crate = "libcrux-kem" }` gives "bans FAILED". The bans are explicit names: `libsignal-protocol`, `libsignal-core`, `libsignal-net`, `libsignal-service`, `libsignal-jni`, `libsignal-ffi`, `signal-crypto`, `zkgroup`, `spqr`, `spqr-syft`, `pq-ratchet`, `libsignal-dezire`, `wacore-libsignal`, `wa-rs-libsignal`; a self-test puts one banned name into a scratch lock and expects "bans FAILED"; `unknown-git = "deny"` stays the main defence.
2. **G-R5 (IMPL-04).** Input: the packages of `cargo tree -e normal --target <shipped triples>`, not everything `cargo vendor` extracts. Rule: SPDX expressions are evaluated (an allowed alternative passes); GPL or AGPL is flagged only where a file declares it as the governing licence; a committed exception file (crate, version, file, reason) starts with `priority-queue` 2.7.0 (`LGPL-3.0-or-later OR MPL-2.0`, MPL elected; LGPL text and headers in every source file) and `crabgrind` 0.2.6 (only under `cfg(valgrind_ct_test)`, never compiled). Negative fixtures: an AGPL header and a "Signal Messenger" copyright.
3. **G-L1 notice sources (IMPL-05).** Many shipped packages publish no licence file (arti-client and the tor-* 0.46.0 crates, every libcrux crate, `hax-lib`, `matrix-pickle`; AndroidX POMs carry only a name and URL). The generator (a GHOST script over `cargo metadata` and the resolved Gradle POMs, adding no Gradle coordinate) takes licence texts from a pinned SPDX license-list-data snapshot for declared ids without a file, and captures upstream `NOTICE` files of Apache-2.0 packages at the published commit (`.cargo_vcs_info.json`) into a committed, reviewed snapshot; the notices file records both sources. The fail condition is "no SPDX id resolvable to a text", not "no licence file"; V-8 checks the snapshot, not the `.crate` contents alone.
4. **G-R8 (IMPL-06 = PH-12).** The rule "empty and equal to the baseline" could not hold: the pinned sources already match (`vz:src/hpke/check_code.rs:79`, a doc comment; `println!("cargo:rustc-cfg=…")` in the build scripts of `libcrux-ml-kem`, `libcrux-sha3` and `libcrux-intrinsics`, the mechanism G-R3 reads; `eprintln!` in `libcrux-platform/src/test.rs`). Rule: scan compiled library code only (no `build.rs`, tests, benches, examples or `cfg(test)` modules, no comments) of every crate of the shipped `ghost-client-dm` graph from `cargo metadata`, with `cargo:` directives matched separately; patterns `SystemTime`, `Instant::now`, `std::time`, `chrono`, `time::`, `log::`, `tracing::`, `println!`, `eprintln!`, `dbg!`; the list must equal a committed baseline whose every entry is justified, and the gate fails on an addition. S0′ freezes the baseline from the real output (V-8).
5. **Pins (IMPL-07; CRY-9 a).** vodozemac 0.11.0 itself depends on `rand 0.10.2`, and `ghost-client-net` already reaches `rand 0.10.2` (directly and through `arti-client`, `tor-basic-utils`) and `rand 0.8.8` (through `num-bigint-dig`), so libcrux-kem adds no `rand` major (RX-10's "second rand major" and §2.3, §2.7, §16.3, ADR-35 point 10 and V-6 are corrected). `rust-crypto-pins.sh` accepts a version set on a `graph` line (with a self-test), and the pins are `curve25519-dalek {4.1.3, 5.0.0}`, `x25519-dalek {2.0.1, 3.0.0}`, `ed25519-dalek {2.2.0, 3.0.0}`, `rand {0.8.8, 0.10.2}` for `ghost-client-net`, plus `hpke 0.14.1`.
6. **G-R6 and the test entry point (IMPL-08).** The deterministic-randomness and vector entry point is a Cargo feature `test-vectors` compiled only into `ghost-dm-host` and a separately named emulator test library packaged only in the `androidTest` APK. G-R6 checks the shipped `libghost_client_net.so` against the closed list, and each test library against the list plus that entry, with `llvm-nm -D --defined-only` (release builds strip `.symtab`, `ghost/Cargo.toml:87-89`). The equivalence claim covers the same source, pins and toolchain with the one feature added; the release smoke test exercises the shipped library.
7. **Deterministic host randomness (IMPL-10).** All DM randomness is drawn inside Rust (`vz:src/types/curve25519.rs:35`, `vz:src/types/ed25519.rs:130,212`; salts, roots, nonces, KEM randomness), ciphertexts decide blob hashes, and Phase 7 orders fetches by blob hash (`InboxStore.kt:188`, then `fetch_seq`, `:313`), so orders differed between the recording run and the crash runs. The `ghost-dm-host` test build uses getrandom 0.4's custom backend (`--cfg getrandom_backend="custom"`, `getrandom-0.4.3/src/backends.rs:11`) backed by a ChaCha20 stream keyed by the world seed and reset per world; S0′ routes every other `getrandom` major in the graph through the same stream; a world's native calls run only on actor threads that world created. G-R2 fails if the cfg reaches an Android build. §15.1's record/replay switch is withdrawn.
8. **Allowlist names (IMPL-11).** G-R1 adds `libcrux-macros`, `libcrux-sha2` and `hax-lib-macros`; S0′ generates the final list from `cargo tree -e normal` on both shipped triples.
9. **`dm-fuzz` (IMPL-12).** cargo-fuzz needs a nightly compiler and a C++ compiler (its README, fetched 2026-09-16): the job pins a dated nightly (`cargo +nightly-YYYY-MM-DD fuzz`); `client-core/dm/fuzz` is excluded from the workspace with its own lock file, checked by cargo-deny; `libfuzzer-sys` is a CI-only dependency (§16.3, ADR-36).
10. **Gate placement (IMPL-13).** `static-gates` has no Rust toolchain or build outputs (`ci.yml:34-46`; `run-all.sh:2`): G-R1, G-R2, G-R4, G-R5, G-R7, G-R8 and the Rust part of G-L1 run in `rust`; G-R3 and G-R6 (Android) in `android-native`, G-R6 (host) where `ghost-dm-host` is built; the Gradle part of G-L1, G-L2 and G-L3 in `android` or `reproducible`; G-L5 and the fixtures stay in `static-gates`.
11. **R8 (IMPL-14).** `-dontobfuscate` has no class filter; the rule is `-keepnames class org.ghost.** { *; }`, and G-DM5 asserts that `mapping.txt` maps every `org.ghost.` class to itself.
12. **Pin syntax (IMPL-16).** `vodozemac = { version = "=0.11.0", default-features = false }`; golden fixtures in `tests/golden/vodozemac-0.11.0/`; a later 0.11.z is a bump under §2.8.
13. **`hpke` 0.14.1 (IMPL-17).** vodozemac 0.11.0 always depends on `hpke ^0.14.1` (not optional; `alloc`, `chacha`, `getrandom`, `x25519`; `MIT/Apache-2.0`) and exports `pub mod hpke` unconditionally (`vz:src/lib.rs:220`); vodozemac 0.10.0 has no such dependency. `hpke` is approved in §16.3 under ADR-35 with an exact pin, and is in the external audit scope unless V-9 shows the linker drops it (GHOST never calls it); Q45 names it.
14. **Dehydrated-pickle check (CRY-8).** `Account::new()` has no fallback key and `Account::fallback_key()` returns only unpublished keys, while the MSC3814 import marks the imported key published (`vz:src/olm/account/mod.rs:381-388,857-865,946-953`; `fallback_keys.rs:62-64`). V-1 calls `generate_fallback_key()` before `to_dehydrated_device`, so the byte comparison covers `opt_fallback_key`, and checks the imported fallback secret behaviourally: an outbound session from a second account to `(OI, F_g)` must open with `create_inbound_session` on the GHOST-built account, and fail with `MissingOneTimeKey` for another F.
15. **Deployment evidence (CRY-9 b).** matrix-rust-sdk `main` enables `insecure-pk-encryption` and `experimental-session-config` with vodozemac's default features (`libolm-compat`, `precomputed-tables`); GHOST's build forbids the first three, so Element's feature configuration differs from GHOST's while the Olm V1 code path is the same at source level (§2.9, §22.1, ADR-35).

#### 22.8.11 Tests, mutants, counts, schema delta, identifiers, questions

- **Mutants.** New: PM-38 (§22.8.7), PM-39 (§22.8.5), MC-31 (§22.8.1), MC-32 (§22.8.4), MC-33 (§22.8.5). Counts: **40 privacy, 32 correctness, 24 ephemeral and envelope**: `dm mutants: 96 run, 96 detected` (97 with MC-30 if Q67's prune is adopted).
- **New cases.** KC3b, `WrapBindingTest`, the low-order and invalid-key vectors of §22.8.2, PQ6, SCN-G week-boundary and post-erasure retries, the T34 one-reply variant, EM-1 late first flights, RO8 and SCN-I variants, the AD-7-with-token attacker and the widened NI-DM6 AD-7 variant, the cargo-deny ban self-test, the `rust-crypto-pins.sh` version-set self-test.
- **Schema delta (migration 5).** `olm_session`: `peer_bind_minute`, `prov_anchor_minute` (minute columns, R-M3), `pair_sent`; trigger `olm_session_identity`: `peer_bind_minute` only increases, `prov_anchor_minute` write-once, `pair_sent` only 0 → 1.
- **New identifiers.** Key kind FETCH; result codes `INVALID_SESSION`, `STATE_CORRUPT`; residue L7-D25; mutants PM-38, PM-39, MC-31 … MC-33; cases KC3b, PQ6, `WrapBindingTest`; S0′ check V-17; questions Q69 … Q71; slice note: the owner actions of §22.8.9 precede S1.
- **Questions.** Q36 re-asked (§22.8.7), Q61 and Q62 amended (§22.8.8, §22.8.9), Q69 (relay visibility floor for bundle fetches), Q70 (reduced reply card), Q71 (distribution channels without the log check).

**Forward note (S0′ evidence, 2026-09-19).** The counts above are the round-9c record and are not rewritten; the conditional they carry is now resolved. S0′ adopted Q67's prune (V-15), so **MC-30 is in the gate and the count is 40 privacy + 33 correctness + 24 ephemeral = `dm mutants: 97 run, 97 detected`** — the number §15.7, §16.1 (`dm-report-check.sh`), §16.2, §17.1 S10 and §17.2 item 8 now carry, and the one `dm-report-check.sh` requires. S0′ also added the JNI functions `chainPosition` (19) and `sessionPrune` (20) to the closed list of §2.6 and to G-R6, and the result `NEW_CHAIN` beside `STATE_CORRUPT` for function 19 (§2.1, §7.7 points 2 and 4).

### 22.9 Forward notes (S4b implementation, 2026-09-20)

**Precedence.** These notes are the record of what slice S4b (the Olm facade, the JNI surface, the
`:crypto-bridge` facade, migration 5, EM-1 and EM-2) delivered where the sections above say
something else. They win over §0–§21 and over §22.0–§22.8 for the items they name, and for nothing
else. The items that carry no round marker are the deviations the first review round left recorded
only in source comments (R2-02); the rest are marked with the round-2 finding they answer. Every
item is implemented and tested on the branch; nothing here is a plan.

| # | Section it corrects | What S4b does, and why |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | §15.2, §17.1, RG-1 ("at most 6 user sends per side") | **EM-1** is enforced at **2** user sends per side on pull requests and **3** nightly (140 075 and 2 296 274 states; the reachable count grows about an order of magnitude per extra send). **EM-2 has no user-send notion at all** — a send is enabled whenever the mode rule yields a mode — so its bound is the number of envelopes in flight: **2** on pull requests, **3** nightly (356 463 and 2 851 029 states, 134 s in the release profile). `GHOST_DM_EM_SENDS` reaches EM-1 and `GHOST_DM_EM_WIRE` reaches EM-2; before round 2 the nightly raised only the first and explored the same EM-2 space as a pull request (S4B2-02) |
| 2 | §17.1 S4b row, Appendix C ("`dm-spike` … deleted in S4b") | `client-core/dm-spike` is **kept**: §15.2's vendor-test substitute runs `dm-spike/scripts/v12-vendor-tests.sh`, and the spike is the verified reference the production crate's identity, session and X-Wing paths are compared against. It is outside the workspace, is not in any shipped graph (G-R1) and is deleted by the S0′ gate slice that moves the vendor-test script to `scripts/ci/` |
| 3 | §2.6 "Typed facade" | `DmSessions` takes **row values** (contact id, the two slots' session ids and wrapped envelopes) instead of `(tx, contact)`: `:crypto-bridge` has no dependency on `:storage` or `:messaging` and must not gain one (§13.1). The transaction rule is unchanged — the caller writes what comes back, inside its own transaction, on `Ok` only. `created` and `renewed` are not outputs: both are GHOST decisions under R-SS1, taken by the layer that owns the two slots (S7) |
| 4 | §2.6 functions 2 and 5 | `ownCard` and `bundleCreate` are exported in **S4b**, not S6: a first flight needs a generation and a peer's pinned Olm identity, and the JVM session tests build both through the real surface rather than reimplementing a derivation |
| 5 | §2.6 function 14 (`sessionInbound`) | **Round 2 (S4B2-01/R2-01).** Every inner decryption failure of a session **creation** is `UNDECRYPTABLE`, the code the row names — including `TooBigMessageGap`, which vodozemac reports *before* the MAC check, and which any holder of the recipient's card could produce with no key material beyond the published fallback key. It used to reach the facade as `GAP_TOO_BIG`, a code `DmSessions.inbound` has no arm for, so an ordinary inbound blob threw `IllegalStateException`: three process deaths per blob before §7.8's poison guard consumed it, against D10 and §7.4's "undecryptable, duplicate, replayed and forged blobs are consumed silently". A gap on an **established** session is still `GAP_TOO_BIG` (function 13) |
| 6 | §2.6 function 7 (`sessionOutbound`) and the typed facade's arms | **Round 2 (S4B2-03/R2-01).** `DmSessions.Outbound` gains `StateCorrupt`, and `NON_CANONICAL` — the JNI layer's answer to a row value or published key of the wrong length — joins `STATE_CORRUPT` on `sessionOutbound`, `encrypt`, `chainPosition` and `sessionPrune`. `INTERNAL` is now literally the only code the facade throws on, as §2.6 states. The code set of each function is the Rust table `jni::FUNCTION_CODES`, and `DmResultCodesTest` compares it with the facade's `when` arms in both directions |
| 7 | §7.9 point 4 ("bytes of pickle") | `SESSION_STATE_MAX` = 65 536 bounds **pickle + sidecar + framing**, which is the arithmetic §7.9 itself uses (39 251 = 36 685 + 2 566, 26 285 B to spare) and what `olm_session.state_env`'s CHECK admits. `OVERSIZE` is reported by `encrypt` alone; the other state-storing functions report `STATE_CORRUPT` |
| 8 | §22.8.2 rule 4 | The libcrux **slice trait** has error types of its own (`KeyGenError`, `EncapsError`, `DecapsError`), not the enum API's single `Error`; each variant is mapped with a wildcard-free `match` and a runtime table test, which is what rule 4 asks for |
| 9 | §2.1 | The pickle reads of `chainPosition` and `sessionPrune` parse with `serde_json` and wipe every value they parsed before dropping the tree. Declared residue, beside the §2.1 deviation: `serde_json`'s own reallocations can leave copies in freed buffers, of the same kind as the library's (L7-D19) |
| 10 | §2.6 function 19 | `chainPosition` is **stricter** than the row: a single unreadable entry among readable ones is `STATE_CORRUPT`, not a silent skip — the layout is one shape for all entries |
| 11 | §16.2 | New nightly job **`dm-state-models`** (EM-1 at three sends per side, EM-2 at three envelopes in flight, release profile), beside the `rust` job's default-bound run |
| 12 | §2.6 "Typed facade", `L1.parse` | `L1` in Kotlin is a **view type** over what `openOuter`/`openIntro` return, not a Kotlin parser: ADR-19 puts no protocol logic in Kotlin, and those two functions arrive with S7 |
| 13 | §3.1, §8.3 | **Round 2 (R2-06).** Every Curve25519 key an L1 carries — the PreKey message's identity, base and one-time key, and either kind's ratchet key — must be the **canonical** encoding of its point (little-endian value below `p = 2^255 − 19`); anything else is `NON_CANONICAL`. RFC 7748 masks the top bit and reduces mod `p`, so `k`, `k \| 2^255` and `k + p` are one point to X25519 and to `curve25519-dalek`'s comparison, while Olm hashes the **bytes** into the session id and matches a receiver chain by them: without the rule one key set named two sessions, and a sender could give its peer a second `olm_session` row and a safety-number mismatch against itself |
| 14 | §2.5, L7-D19 | **Round 2 (R2-04).** Every byte array crossing the JNI boundary is copied into a `Zeroizing` buffer, so `ctxOpen`'s MESSAGING branch, `ghost1` seed and `session_state_key`, and every L0 plaintext, are erased when the call returns instead of surviving `ctxClose` in freed native memory |
| 15 | §16.1 G-R2, G-R4, G-R7 | **Round 2 (R2-03).** G-R2 is implemented in `rust-feature-policy.sh`: vodozemac in the shipped client graph without `libolm-compat`, `low-level-api`, `experimental-session-config` or `insecure-pk-encryption`, `ghost-client-dm` with no feature but `jni-bridge`, and both crates required to be in that graph so the clause cannot hold vacuously; three self-test fixtures. **Deferred to the S0′ gate slice, with G-R3 and G-R8:** G-R4's dependency-graph half (`no-logging.sh` already covers its source half over `client-core`, including this crate) and G-R7's **script**, whose content S4b already carries as tests — `tests/golden.rs` pins the golden pickles, the transcripts and the `chainPosition` and `sessionPrune` outputs per pin, and the wildcard-free error matches are its compile-time half; what the script adds is the two-directory comparison at a bump, and the first pin is exempt by G-R7's own rule |
| 16 | §15.2 property tests | **Round 2 (S4B2-04).** The S4b parsers run the `10^6` seeded inputs of R-C4: `olm::message::parse`/`decode` over mutated real PreKey and normal messages, and `SessionState::decode`/`Sidecar::decode` over mutated real states. The pickle readers reached through `chainPosition`, `sessionPrune` and `encrypt` run `10^3`, for the reason `property.rs` gives the signature-verifying parsers: one input costs a `serde_json` parse of a whole session pickle |
| 17 | §15.2 EM-1 properties | **Round 2 (R2-07).** "No message lost on a session the device held" had no mutant and could not fail; `Selection::DropsHeldMessage` is the receiver that breaks it, run through the search like MC-19. The JVM V-10 / MC-29 test now forges with another pair's normal message — the unknown ratchet key that reaches the sending ratchet's activation — instead of one on a chain the receiver already held |

### 22.10 Forward notes (S5 implementation, 2026-09-20)

**Precedence.** These notes are the record of what slice S5 (the v5 trigger transition tests, the
R-M1, R-M3 and R-M4 introspection tests, the pickle structure test and the DAO lint) delivered
where the sections above say something else, and of **what it deliberately did not deliver**. They
win over §0–§21 and over §22.0–§22.9 for the items they name, and for nothing else. Items 1 and 3
are **deferrals**: they name work that S5 could not honestly do because its dependencies have not
landed, say exactly what is missing, and say what S6, S7 and S9 must add. Everything else on the
list is implemented and tested on the branch.

| # | Section it corrects | What S5 does, and why |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | §13.6 R-M1, §17.1 S5 row | **Deferred in part: R-M1 is proved at the schema level, not end to end.** `DmForensicResidueTest` (`:storage`) builds a world holding every row kind a live conversation can hold — `dm_scheduled` among them, which §12 writes and no deletion of §10.6 removes — beside a contact still in `request` past its deadline and one whose `previous` session is due for retirement, and issues **eight** of the nine deletions §10.6 lists as SQL: expired messages, held rows past `hold_until_minute`, `dm_seen` rows past `until_day`, requests past `request_until_day` (walking the widest cascade R-M1 depends on: contact, conversation, messages, session, pair keys and offer, peer key, held row, scheduled control, outbound item, pending send and bundle candidate), pair week keys past their freshness, retired `previous` sessions at `retire_day`, `prov_env` past `prov_erase_minute`, and generations past `delete_after_day` with their reuse tuples. (The ninth, surplus `dm_peer_pq` rows, is bounded by a count and by the `dm_peer_pq_limit` trigger, not by a time, so it is not a sweep this test can issue.) It then asserts the dump three ways. `dm_message`, `dm_reaction`, `dm_outbound_item`, `dm_outbound_op`, `dm_pending_send` and `dm_held` are empty, most of them as cascades of the schema and not as deletes the test issued. Every byte string the messages carried is searched for **inside** every BLOB value of every table and not only compared to it, so a body embedded in a larger envelope is found. And the surviving columns are asserted over the **whole** dump rather than a slice of four tables: the set of tables that still holds a row is pinned against the sentence that permits each, and every column of every one of those tables is either allowed residue or declared cleared with its reason, both checked against `PRAGMA table_info`, so a column added to a surviving table later is in neither list and fails. That last assertion is what found the two residues **no declaration named before S5** — `dm_bundle_candidate`, a 1421-byte cached peer bundle per contact, and `dm_scheduled`, a pending control with a payload and a future minute — which L7-D7 now names. §10.6's rule that a generation is not deleted while a live `dm_held` row references it is **code, not schema** (`dm_held.gen_id` carries a range CHECK and no `REFERENCES`), and the test pins that fact so S9's SWEEP keeps the ordering. **What is missing** is the behavioural half: the rows are seeded with SQL because the DAOs that create them are **S6 and S7**, and the deletions are issued by the test because the step that issues them is **SWEEP, slice S9**. A DAO that forgets to write `delete_at_minute`, or a SWEEP that misses a row kind, is not covered here. Nor is the **physical** half: the dump is logical, so a page of a freed `-wal` frame is outside its reach and belongs to the raw `.db`/`-wal` scan §10.9 point 2 assigns to the T25 EP cases. S9 must run this assertion again with the real SWEEP transaction driving it (the `DmWorld` RE and EP suites of S10 are where it lands), and S7 must not add a row kind to a conversation without adding it to this test's world |
| 2 | §13.6 R-M3 ("the random ids `gen_id`, `peer_gen_id` are integers with `typeof` CHECKs") | **Recorded deviation.** Four of the five columns named `gen_id`/`peer_gen_id` carry the CHECK (`dm_held`, `olm_prekey_gen`, `dm_peer_pq`, `olm_session`). **`olm_prekey_used.gen_id` does not**, and migration 5 is not reopened to add it: the column is a foreign key to `olm_prekey_gen.gen_id`, which has the CHECK, and it has INTEGER affinity, so SQLite stores `'7'` and `7.0` as the integer 7 and leaves only a value with a fractional part un-coerced — which then matches no parent row and is refused by the foreign key. `DmSchemaIntrospectionTest.theReuseTupleInheritsItsTypeFromItsParentGeneration` executes both halves (`7.0` is stored as `typeof = 'integer'`; `7.5` is `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed`), and the exemption is enumerated, asserted to be used, and asserted to really be a foreign key. A migration that ever drops that foreign key must add the `typeof` CHECK in the same statement |
| 3 | §10.7 point 4, §21.14 ("the expired-row lint over every DAO query"), mutant ME-16 | **Deferred in part: the lint mechanism is built and armed; its corpus is empty because no DM DAO exists.** `:messaging` is an `AndroidManifest.xml` and nothing else, and `:storage` has no DM repository, so there is no read path to lint. S5 therefore builds the **mechanism**, in `:storage`'s `DmExpiredRowLint` / `DmExpiredRowLintTest`, with both halves §10.7 point 4 implies. (a) **Static:** every `src/main` Kotlin and Java source under the Android root is scanned **at any depth** — a nested module (`features/dm/src/main`) must not go unscanned while the other modules still satisfy the vacuity floor, which is the rule `dm-no-fts.sh`'s recursive `find` already applied — for SQL that `SELECT`s from an expiring table. **Adjacent literals joined by `+` are concatenated before matching**, because that is how this repo writes long SQL (`OutboxStore`, `DirectoryStore`, `StateStore` all put the `SELECT` list in one literal and the `FROM` clause in the next); a per-literal test would find `SELECT` without the table in one half and the table without `SELECT` in the other, and see nothing. `CREATE TRIGGER`/`TABLE`/`INDEX` are skipped, with `dm_message_no_replace` as the pinned reason, and writes are skipped because SWEEP's own `DELETE … WHERE delete_at_minute <= ?` is supposed to name expired rows; **`CREATE VIEW` is deliberately not skipped**, because a view over `dm_message` is a read path and a DAO selecting from it would name no expiring table at all, so skipping it would bypass both halves end to end. Every query found must be registered in `DmDaoQueries`; **today the found set and the registry are both empty, and the test asserts it scanned at least 100 real sources (140 today) including `Schema.kt`, so "empty" cannot be a broken scanner**. The one shape the scan still cannot see is a table name that arrives through a template or a constant (`"SELECT … FROM $DM_MESSAGE"`), and a DM DAO therefore spells the table out. (b) **Behavioural:** each registered query is run against **two** databases that differ only in the expired `dm_message` rows — the fixture world with them, and the same world after SWEEP removed them — and the two result sets must be **identical**: a compliant read path cannot tell whether SWEEP has run yet. Comparing whole result sets, rather than looking for the expired `message_id` in the first column, is what makes an aggregate fail — `SELECT count(*) …` without the filter is an unread badge counting an expired message, which is ME-16 itself and which no id-shaped check would see — and it also covers a query that projects the id second or returns another table's columns. Beside it, a registered query that matches no row even in the swept world fails as **broken rather than compliant**, which is the shape (an inbound or unread read path with arguments the world does not match) that would otherwise pass for free. Six near misses are proved to be caught: `>=` for `>` at the boundary, the filter on `unread_deadline_minute`, the filter on `expire_at_minute`, the filter ORed instead of ANDed, the bare count, and the id projected second. The world holds three expired and two live rows with a live and an expired one in **each** direction, and the three expiry columns deliberately diverge across them (an expired read incoming message whose unread deadline is still in the future, an expired unread incoming message whose `expire_at_minute` is NULL), so a wrong-column filter is caught by a value and not by a NULL. **What S6/S7 must do:** the day a DM DAO reads `dm_message`, `everyProductionReadOfAnExpiringTableIsRegistered` fails with the file and line; register the query **verbatim** in `DmDaoQueries` with its arguments, and `everyRegisteredQueryHidesAnExpiredRow` then runs it. **Scope:** the expiring table is `dm_message` and the column is `delete_at_minute`, which is what §10.7 point 4 names; the other rows SWEEP deletes by time (held envelopes, seen tags, pair keys, generations, retired sessions) reach no view, so their bound is the sweep itself. If S7 gives one of them a read path a user sees, it joins `DmExpiredRowLint.expiringTables` then |
| 4 | §13.6 R-M3 ("a test lists every column of every table") | The three introspection tests that existed before S5 each looked at their own tables — `:sync`'s `SchemaIntrospectionTest` at the 15 sync columns, `EntitlementSchemaIntrospectionTest` at the 14 entitlement ones, `Migration5Test` at the 24 that migration 5 adds — so a time column added to a table none of them owned was invisible to all three. `DmSchemaIntrospectionTest` takes its corpus from `sqlite_master` instead, so a table created by a future migration is covered the day it exists. Its rule widens the Phase 7 name test with R-M3's own words (`minute`, `hour`, `day`, `week`), and every match must be in one of four declared sets: the migration 4 and 5 enumeration, the columns v1/v2/v3 already carried (listed, not exempted — the next `last_activity_minute` must not be able to land one table to the left, mutant PM-10), the period indices and random ids, or the justified non-times (`minute_index`, `timer_code`, `timer_version`, `ttl_seconds`, `channels.current_epoch`). All four are asserted in both directions, and each exemption must still match something. The third set is a deliberate **superset** of R-M3's own enumeration, and this is the record of why: beside the columns R-M3 names it carries the three `ent_*` epochs and `ent_purchase.base_week`, which are the v3 grid indices under the same rule since §19.17, and `dm_pair_key.root_gen` and `dm_pair_offer.root_gen`, 0..65535 pair-root generation indices that migration 5 gives the same `typeof` CHECK while R-M3's sentence omits them — a set that stopped at R-M3's words would leave those six CHECKs with nothing enforcing them. Beside reading the CHECKs out of `CREATE TABLE` as text, `everyTypedColumnRefusesAFractionalValue` **executes** every one of them: for each of the 46 v4 and v5 columns a REAL whose whole part is aligned (`x.5`) must be refused and the same value written `x.0` must be stored as an integer, and wherever the schema writes a `% N = 0` the divisor is read out of the column's own text and a value one unit off the grid must be refused too (which is what covers `dm_seen.until_day`'s `% 7 = 0`, the one granularity the `_day` branch of the text assertion only looks for as "some CHECK"). A text assertion alone cannot tell a CHECK that is present from one that is vacuous — `CHECK (typeof(x) = 'integer' AND x BETWEEN 0 AND 65535 OR 1)` parses as `(A AND B) OR 1`, refuses nothing, and leaves the substring intact. Two column pairs are written fractional on both sides because a CHECK ties them (`dm_message`'s `expire_at_minute`/`delete_at_minute`, `olm_prekey_gen`'s `superseded_day`/`delete_after_day`), since writing one as a REAL and the other as an integer would be refused by the tying CHECK and prove nothing; the four v3 grid indices are executed by `EntitlementSchemaConstraintsTest.timesAndGridIndicesAreWholeIntegers` and named here as delegated |
| 5 | §16.1 G-DM6, §10.7 "No FTS" | `dm-no-fts.sh` is implemented and runs in `run-all.sh`. **Production SQL** is every Kotlin and Java source on the Android production path (`*/src/main/*`) plus every `*.sql` file of the monorepo outside build outputs and test trees; test sources are excluded because a test may name FTS to prove it is absent, and `Migration5Test` does. The `fts` token is matched with a leading token boundary and no trailing one (`fts`, `FTS5`, `fts_content`, `ftsIndex` are reported; `shifts`, `drafts`, `lifts` are not), and **a comment that merely mentions FTS in a production source fails too**, deliberately: the rule is that the token does not appear, and a gate that had to tell a comment from a statement would be a SQL parser with a parser's blind spots. The gate refuses to pass vacuously twice over — at least one source must be scanned, and at least one of them must contain a `CREATE TABLE`, so the day the schema moves out of `android/*/src/main` the gate fails instead of going quiet. `CREATE VIRTUAL TABLE` is matched over each file's **newline-squashed** text and not line by line, because `grep` is line oriented and every DDL statement of `Schema.kt` is a multi-line raw string, so `CREATE VIRTUAL\n TABLE` — the house style — would otherwise pass; a file that does hold one is then grepped line by line so the report still carries a line number. R-M4 forbids the statement outright and not only its FTS modules, so a line-split `rtree` or a custom module is caught by this pass and not by the token check. Five single-purpose self-test fixtures (`negative-dm-fts-virtual`, `-virtual-split`, `-token`, `-sql-file`, `-no-sql`) plus an empty tree prove each half on its own. The schema half of R-M4 is `DmSchemaIntrospectionTest.noObjectOfTheSchemaIsOrNamesAVirtualTable`, which reads **every** `sqlite_master` row (an index, a trigger or a view could name a virtual table as well as a table can) |
| 6 | §13.6 R-M3 ("the pickle structure test") | `client-core/dm/tests/pickle_structure.rs` decodes a wrapped state, parses the pickle and enumerates every field of every object at every depth as a `path : json-type`; the set must **equal** `PINNED_SCHEMA` in both directions — an added field is the drift T33 exists to catch, and a field that disappeared means the schema has stopped describing anything, which is also how `chainPosition` and `sessionPrune` would lose the four paths they read by name (§2.1). The corpus is every shape a stored session can take: the golden file's three states of the pinned version (initiator, responder with four skipped keys, responder pruned) and five live states (outbound before and after its first message, inbound before and after its reply, initiator after receiving), so **both** sending-ratchet shapes (`active`, `inactive`) and a chain carrying skipped keys are represented. Beside the field set: the enum tags are pinned by value (`config.version = V1`, `sending_ratchet.type ∈ {active, inactive}`), no field name may contain a time word — with `session_keys.one_time_key` the one declared, asserted-used exemption, since it is the recipient's one-time prekey — and the mechanism itself is proved on a deliberately drifted pickle rather than assumed. A bump changes `PINNED_SCHEMA` in the same commit as the regenerated goldens (§2.8, G-R7), and that diff is the record of what the new version stores |
| 7 | §17.1 S5 row ("trigger, **guard** … tests") | The REPLACE guards of v5 needed no new work in S5 and none was written: `ReplaceGuardsTest.everyGuardCoversEveryKeyOfItsTableAndOnlyTwoV2TablesGoWithout` already checks every guard's key coverage, and `Migration5Test` exercises the guards of `dm_self`, `dm_contact`, `olm_session`, `olm_prekey_gen` and `olm_prekey_used` behaviourally, including the partial unique index of `olm_prekey_gen`. What S5 adds instead is the **transition matrix** the walks in `Migration5Test` cannot give: `Migration5TriggerTransitionsTest` attempts all `6 × 6` ordered pairs of `dm_contact.trust_state` and all `7 × 7` of `dm_message.state`, requiring each legal edge to be accepted and every other to be refused by name — a trigger that lost an arm passes a walk through the arms it kept and cannot pass a matrix — and covers the four v5 rules no walk reached: `dm_conversation_timer`, the incoming half of `dm_message_expiry`, `olm_session`'s write-once `unack_since_day` and erase-only `prov_env`, and `olm_prekey_gen`'s write-once `delete_after_day`. The same idea is then applied to the **immutability** triggers, which are not state machines but disjunctions of arms and which a walk through one arm passes after the others are gone: `dm_self_fixed`, `dm_contact_identity`, `dm_message_identity`, `dm_pair_offer_root`, `olm_session_identity` and `olm_prekey_gen_fixed` each get the **frozen-column matrix** — every column of the table is attempted, the frozen ones must be refused **by that trigger's own message** (a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger runs before CHECKs and foreign keys, so neither can stand in for a lost arm), the changeable ones must be accepted so the trigger cannot be over-wide, and every column must be in one of three declared sets (frozen, changeable, or ruled elsewhere with the reason and where), checked against `PRAGMA table_info` so a column added later is in none of them and fails. That is what reaches the arms no walk did: `dm_self`'s `id`, `dm_contact`'s `contact_id`, both of `dm_pair_offer`'s `offer_env` arms, `olm_prekey_gen`'s `gen_id` and `fallback_pub`, `olm_session`'s `peer_gen_id`, write-once `prov_anchor_minute`, `pair_sent` and decreasing `peer_bind_minute`, and `dm_message_expiry`'s `expire_at_minute → NULL`. On the REPLACE guards the claim above is **one arm each**: `Migration5Test` fires one key of each guard it exercises, and every guard's full key coverage is checked as **text** by `ReplaceGuardsTest.everyGuardCoversEveryKeyOfItsTableAndOnlyTwoV2TablesGoWithout` (`sql.contains("NEW.$key")`) and not behaviourally — `olm_session`'s second arm, `dm_conversation`'s `contact_id` arm, `dm_own_namespace`'s `(role, card_index)` arm and `dm_scheduled`'s `(contact_id, what)` arm are covered structurally only |

### 22.11 Forward notes (S6 implementation, 2026-09-20)

**Precedence.** These notes are the record of what slice S6 (ACTIVATE with the `:entitlement` K20
hook, the own namespaces, OWNER-WEEK, KEEPER, REGROWS, `myCard` and the G-DM2 gate) delivered where
the sections above say something else, and of **what it deliberately did not deliver**. They win
over §0–§21 and over §22.0–§22.10 for the items they name, and for nothing else. Items 6, 7, 8 and
9 are **deferrals**: they name work that S6 could not honestly do because its dependencies have not
landed, say exactly what is missing, and say which slice must add it. Items 14–18 were added by the
**review of S6 (2026-09-20)**, which found four defects in what this list already claimed — a
missed week that published a week late, a healing rule that was dead in the pass order the device
really runs, a REGROWS that crashed for one to two weeks after an ES slot-table change, and a step
that committed half its work while reporting an error — and one dead read with a misleading
contract; items 3, 7, 8 and 11 are corrected in place for the same reasons. Everything else on the list
is implemented and tested on the branch.

| # | Section it corrects | What S6 does, and why |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | §2.6 function 4 (`ownReadSecret`), §3.2 | **The function returns three secrets, not one.** §2.6's output column says `RX_{i,s}`; the exported function returns `RX_{i,s}`, `D_{i,s}` and `RP_{i,s}` of the same `(card index, slot)`. REGROWS has to register a verifier for all three kinds of the owner's own namespaces (§4.8: `X`: DEPOSIT and READ; `P`: FETCH), and all three are bearer secrets §3.2's closing sentence lets Kotlin hold — they are precisely the bytes that go on the wire inside a credential v3. The alternative was to export `cardDerive` (function 3) early, but its other half is contact-card parsing, which is the S7 material this slice must not build; three secrets in one call is also one fewer JNI crossing than three calls, and the caller needs them together. The key inputs stay typed (T26, G-DM3): the MESSAGING branch for `RX`, the card secret for the other two. Each is a `Zeroizing` copy that is wiped before the call returns (R2-04) |
| 2 | §2.6 function 2 (`ownCard`), §5.4 | **`ownCard` also returns the weekly publication offset** `t_pub(w) − start(w)`, as a `u32` of seconds already floored to the minute. §5.4 puts `t_pub` in the owner's scheduler, which is `:messaging`'s OWNER-WEEK, while the schedule key `S_i` it is derived from must not leave the native context — it is not one of the three things §3.2 lets Kotlin hold. So the offset crosses and the key does not. **The convention §5.4 left open is fixed here:** the 32-byte HMAC becomes an integer as its **first 8 bytes, big-endian**, reduced modulo 604 800 and floored to the minute. The modulo bias is about one part in `3·10^13` and taking the whole digest as a 256-bit integer would change no observable while needing a bignum reduction in a crate that has none. `derive::publication_offset` and its unit test pin the transcript; the new TLV tags are `PublicationOffset = 0x18`, `DepositSecret = 0x19`, `FetchSecret = 0x1a`, beside `ReadSecret = 0x17`, and `DmResultCodesTest` keeps the Rust and Kotlin tag lists equal in both directions |
| 3 | §13.1, §13.3, §4.8 | **Three small `:sync` API additions, each because an owner step could not otherwise keep its own guard.** (a) `Capabilities.installed(tx, relay, namespace, kind)` returns the installed row's state and floored expiry hour, without its token: §4.8 refreshes the owner's own READ credential "when fewer than 7 days remain", so KEEPER has to read back what it installed, and a copy kept in memory would be lost at every restart and would re-put — and therefore bump the capability generation and re-arm every parked delivery — on every process start. `CapabilityNeed.Reason.EXPIRING` could not stand in: it fires at 24 hours, not at 7 days, and `needed()` opens a transaction of its own. (b) `Registrations.request` now returns `Boolean`, true only when it inserted the row, so REGROWS — whose whole contract is "inserts only missing rows" (§7.2) — can report that it inserted none without reading `relay_key_registration` from `:messaging`, which would cross the module boundary §13.1 draws. (c) `InstalledCapability` is the new value type of (a); it deliberately carries no token. **Added by the review of S6 (2026-09-20):** (d) `Registrations.keep(tx, relay, ns, period, kind, verifier)` is `request` plus the slot-table change of §5.2 — see item 14 — and is what REGROWS calls; `request` keeps its strict "a registration keeps the verifier it was created with" and stays the primitive. (e) `Registrations.pending(tx, relay, ns, fromPeriod)` counts one pair's unfinished rows inside the caller's transaction, which is what bounds KEEPER's re-put of a refused READ credential — see item 15 |
| 4 | §13.1 `:crypto-bridge`, §5.2, §4.3 | **`CredentialV3` and `RelaySets` take raw bytes, not `:sync` types.** §13.1 lists both in `:crypto-bridge`, and §22.9 item 3 fixed the rule that this module keeps **no** production dependency; `RelaySets.Slot` therefore carries the relay as the caller's `Long` handle and the operator as bytes, and `:messaging` maps them to `RelayId`. Two rules the design states in prose are fixed in code here: the slot order compares the 32 HMAC bytes as **unsigned**, with the slot number as the tiebreak, because without a total order two devices could compute different sets from one ES and lose each other's mail; and §5.2's stopping rule is read as the conjunction it is written as, so three relays of a single operator continue the walk — but only to the next slot of a **new** operator, so a week whose slots are all one operator yields three relays and not the whole table. Both are checked against an independently written HMAC-SHA256 over `MessageDigest` rather than against the same `javax.crypto.Mac` the implementation uses |
| 5 | §7.2 ACTIVATE row, §5.4 | **ACTIVATE claims `dm_self.bundle_week` as well.** §7.2 lists `dm_self.bundle_week` under OWNER-WEEK and not under ACTIVATE, but an activation publishes a bundle for week `w`, so a time-triggered pass a minute later would otherwise rotate the generation again inside the same week. The two steps share the one guard `bundle_week < w`, which is what makes "OWNER-WEEK per week" hold across an activation |
| 6 | §5.3 (`bundle_rank`), §7.2 ACTIVATE row | **Deferred in part: the off-schedule rank is always 1, never `1 + max(rank)`.** §5.3 says an ACTIVATE or restore publication in week `w` uses `1 + max(rank)` over the valid bundles of weeks `w − 1 … w + 1` **that the device lists in its own `P_0`**, "or 1 if none". That listing is a relay read: it cannot happen inside the ACTIVATE transaction, and no read path for a fetched own bundle exists before S7's `BUNDLE` step. S6 therefore takes the "or 1 if none" branch unconditionally. **What is right about it:** rank 1 beats the rank 0 of every OWNER-WEEK bundle, so a restored device's bundle wins over the one its lost device published that week, which is the case the rule exists for. **What is wrong about it:** a **second** off-schedule publication inside one week — an activation and a restore, or two restores — publishes at rank 1 as well, and fetchers then break the tie by the smaller blob hash instead of by recency, so the older of the two may win for the rest of that week. The window is bounded by the week, and the loser's next OWNER-WEEK publication at `t_pub(w + 1)` ends it. **What S7 must add:** with `BUNDLE` in place, read the candidates of `P_0` before the ACTIVATE transaction and pass `1 + max(rank)` in; the `rank` argument of `publishBundle` is already the seam, and `FakeDmCrypto.requestedRanks` is where the assertion goes |
| 7 | §7.2 KEEPER row | **Deferred in part: KEEPER installs only the owner's own READ credential.** §7.2's KEEPER row also names "contacts' deposit and bundle read credentials per relay slot, refreshed before expiry, a refused one re-put at most once per session start". No contact exists before `addContact`, which is S7, and a contact's credentials are **card-derived**, which needs `cardDerive` (§2.6 function 3, `Declared("S7")` on the closed list). What S6 implements is the owner half of §4.8 in full: the 8-day credential v3 per relay of `X_0`'s set, refreshed inside its last 7 days, re-put when the installed one is rejected, and left untouched otherwise — "KEEPER puts only a differing token". The `setRelays` half is complete for both own namespaces, including the reader's 6-week union of §5.2. **On "a refused one re-put at most once per relay session start" (§21.2), corrected by the review of S6 (2026-09-20):** the first version of this note argued that `Capabilities.put` setting the row back to `usable` was bound enough. It is not, and it also broke §4.8's healing rule — see item 15. The bound now comes from the registrations: while the pair has a pending row the credential is **not** re-put and reads use the write capability, which is §21.2 point 8 read literally, so a re-put costs one registration round trip rather than one `CAPABILITIES` hint (mutant MC-15). **What S7 must add:** the contact loop, and the same bound wherever a contact credential is re-put after a refusal |
| 8 | §16.1 G-DM2, §17.1 S6 row | **The gate is built and armed; its forbidden side is proved only against fixtures.** `dm-enqueue-callers.sh` reads every production source of `android/messaging` and `android/app` and refuses the restricted calls from any file not on its allowlist, and `Namespaces.remove` from any file not on the SWEEP-extended one. **Widened by the review of S6 (2026-09-20)**, which found the first list of six short by four calls S6's own owner steps already make or that are as relay-visible as the six: the set is `Outbox.enqueue`, `Outbox.cancel`, `Registrations.request`, `Registrations.keep`, `Registrations.rearm`, `Registrations.forget`, `Namespaces.register`, `Namespaces.setRelays`, `Namespaces.setListening`, `Namespaces.setSendDelay` and `Capabilities.put`. The same review fixed two more things: `Sweeper.kt` was on the main allowlist as well as on the remove-only one, which made the "may call `Namespaces.remove` **only**" the script states unenforceable (it is now on the remove-only list alone), and a call site was recognized only when its receiver sat on the same physical line — a receiver on the line above, a local alias (`val ob = sync.outbox`) and a scope function over a port (`with(sync.outbox) { enqueue(…) }`) all passed. Lines are now folded on their leading `.`, aliases are tracked per file, and a file that scopes over a port has its bare calls matched; what still escapes — an alias through a parameter, a field or a collection, and reflection — is written into the script's header, where `DmExpiredRowLint` records its own blind spots. **A third rule was added by the same review:** the call-site rule alone said nothing about who may *reach* those calls, and `engine.runOwnerMaintenance()` on an inbound blob walks into OWNER-WEEK and `Outbox.enqueue` while the restricted call stays inside the allowlisted `OwnerSteps.kt` — mutants PM-11 and PM-30, a bundle a minute after a card holder's probe. `Messaging.runOwnerMaintenance`, `onActivated` and `onRestored` may therefore appear only in the two files that declare and implement them and in the two that may start a pass: the app wiring (a database open, the K20 hook) and the DM actor (its timer and the relay session start, S7). The allowlist names the files that **may** call, so a file that does not exist yet is refused by default rather than admitted by default, which is what makes the S7 files arrive deliberately. It runs in `run-all.sh` and refuses to pass vacuously twice over: at least one DM production source must be found, and at least one of them must really make a restricted call, so the day `OwnerSteps.kt` is renamed the gate fails instead of going quiet. **What is not exercisable today:** the real violation. No `ReceiveStep`, `BundleStep` or `HeldReplay` exists to call one of these functions wrongly, so the forbidden side is proved by six single-purpose fixtures in `test-harness/gates/` — a RECV that registers a namespace because a blob arrived, a store-layer `Namespaces.remove`, a RECV that hides its three call sites behind a line break, an alias and a scope function, a SWEEP that does more than remove a namespace, a RECV that reaches the enqueue through `runOwnerMaintenance()` instead, and a tree whose DM sources make no restricted call at all — plus an empty tree. `NoReflexTest`, the behavioural half §16.1 names, is **S7's**: it needs two worlds that differ only in what arrived. **What S7 must do:** add its step files to the allowlist with the transactions they hold, and write `NoReflexTest`; the day a RECV, BUNDLE or HELD file makes one of these calls, this gate names the file and the line |
| 9 | §13.1 `…messaging.android`, §13.7, §16.1 | **Three more pieces this slice leaves to the slice that owns them, recorded so they are not lost.** (a) **The DM actor.** §13.7 makes one thread the only DM writer; §17.1 puts it in the S7 row with the listener hand-off and the `ConsumerTail` body, and S6 has not built it. S6's writers are serialized by `SyncDatabase`'s own transaction lock, which is what §13.7 relies on underneath; the actor adds ordering *between* transactions, which no owner step of this slice needs. (b) **The Keystore wrappers.** `KeystoreContentKeyWrapper` and `KeystoreStateKeyWrapper` of §13.1 are S12's (`MessagingWiring`); S6 ships the `KeyWrapper` port and the in-memory implementation §13.8 names for JVM tests, and the schema's 40..256-byte CHECK on both wrapped columns is what pins the shape until then. (c) **The gate scopes of §16.1's last rows.** `sync-no-catch-all.sh`, `no-logging.sh`, `anti-placeholder.sh` and `kotlin-clearnet.sh` are to cover `android/messaging`, `android/crypto-bridge` and `client-core/dm`; S6 did not widen them, because `:crypto-bridge`'s `DmSessions.call` catches `UnsatisfiedLinkError` by design (§2.7: a missing library is `CRYPTO_UNAVAILABLE`, never an `Error` escaping into `:messaging`) and `sync-no-catch-all.sh`'s pattern refuses every `*Error`. §16.1 assigns that widening to the S0′ gate slice ("S0′ checks each script's file set and extends it where needed"), which must decide between an exemption for that one call and a rule that names it. `messaging-no-network.sh` (G-DM1) is not written either and belongs with it |
| 10 | §13.2 | **The facade is the S6 subset of §13.2's interface, not a stub of the whole.** `Messaging` declares `myCard`, `onActivated`, `onRestored` and `runOwnerMaintenance`; the methods that need a contact arrive with S7, trust and restore with S8, the ephemeral calls with S9. Each signature lands with its slice rather than as a method that returns nothing, so a caller cannot compile against something that does not work (`anti-placeholder.sh`). `runOwnerMaintenance` is new and is not in §13.2: it is the ordered pass §13.7 describes for the time-triggered steps — OWNER-WEEK, then KEEPER, then REGROWS, in that order, because OWNER-WEEK may move the bundle week and KEEPER must then install credentials for the set as it now is |
| 11 | §13.4 (K20), §21.12 point 2 | **The hook is a prompt; resumable activation is the guarantee.** `IdentityActivationHook` is an interface in `:entitlement`'s api and an implementation in `:messaging`, so the module graph of §13.1 keeps its direction, and `EngineDeps` takes it as a **supplier** because `:app` wires the two engines in one order and the hook in the other. It is called after `c.identity.create(...)` in both trial paths (`activate` and the crash-recovery `resume`) and after `c.identity.restore(...)`. Nothing is caught around it: `sync-no-catch-all.sh` refuses a catch-all in `android/entitlement/src/main`, and the crash-fidelity rule of the engine is that an unexpected throwable reaches the top level. The hook's own contract is that it does not throw — `Messaging.onActivated()` answers with an enum, `CRYPTO_UNAVAILABLE` included — and the safety net is §21.12 point 2, which runs ACTIVATE again at every actor start and database open. **Genesis is not hooked:** §13.4 names "the activation finalization (trial and genesis)", and the genesis path creates no identity inside `:entitlement`'s engine, so there is no call site there; S12's `MessagingWiring` must call `onActivated()` on that path, or rely on the resumable activation. **Raised by the review of S6 (2026-09-20), not a defect today:** `onRestored()` answers `ALREADY_ACTIVE` when a `dm_self` row exists, so a restore onto a database that still held a previous identity's row would set no `accept_after_minute` and publish no bundle. `RestoreScan.restore` refuses outright while `identity.hasIdentity()`, and no path in the tree removes an identity while keeping the DM tables, so the state cannot be reached in S6. **S8 owns it:** the restore slice must either wipe `dm_self` with the identity or make ACTIVATE recognize a row of another identity |
| 12 | §5.2, ADR-11 | **A week that cannot satisfy the replica rule rolls the whole step back.** `RelaySets.forNamespace` returns what the week's table can give and refuses nothing itself; the refusal is `Outbox.enqueue`'s `InsufficientReplicasException`, as §5.2 says. Because ACTIVATE's enqueue is inside its one transaction, a single-operator week leaves **no** `dm_self`, no generation and no half-registered namespace, and §21.12 point 2's resumable activation runs it again later. That is asserted rather than assumed |
| 13 | §4.8, graft G-3, judge error E-8 | **REGROWS keeps the `{c, c + 1}` rows too, not only `week(now)`.** §4.8 asks for both, and the `c + 1` row is what buys the offline tolerance E-8 names: an owner online in week `w` registers `{w, w + 1}`, so a sender — which presents one static credential with no week choice and no fallback (§21.2) — keeps depositing until `start(c + 3) + 1 h`, about two to three weeks of absence. `c` is read from the pair's usable write capability's **declared expiry**, the same way §4.4 point 3 has the relay judge the period, so client and relay agree without either reading the other's clock; a capability whose expiry is unknown names no week and contributes nothing. Periods before `w − 1` are filtered out rather than inserted, because the same pass deletes them |
| 14 | §4.8, §5.2, §21.2 point 2 | **Added by the review of S6 (2026-09-20): REGROWS replaces a row whose slot moved under it, instead of aborting.** A verifier binds the relay's **slot number**, a relay may keep a namespace's set while moving to another of its slots (§5.2, §21.1 point 1), and item 13's `{c, c + 1}` rows are of earlier weeks — so a row whose verifier was computed at the old slot is one nobody can present any more. `Registrations.request`'s "a registration keeps the verifier it was created with" made that an `IllegalStateException` that left the transaction, aborting REGROWS **and the `forget` loop that would have dropped the offending rows**, at every pass until the periods lapsed — one to two weeks in which the whole owner maintenance pass died with it. `Registrations.keep` deletes the stale row and inserts a fresh pending one with the new verifier; the row itself still never changes its verifier (the `relay_key_registration_state` trigger refuses that), and the relay needs the new record anyway, since `RegisterKey` records accumulate per verifier (SC-1) |
| 15 | §4.8 healing rule, §21.2 point 8, mutants MC-14 and MC-15 | **Added by the review of S6 (2026-09-20): the healing of a refused own READ credential is KEEPER's, not REGROWS's.** §4.8 states the rule under REGROWS and §21.2 point 8 under the owner's credential; in the pass order §13.7 gives (OWNER-WEEK, KEEPER, REGROWS) only the second can work, and the first was dead code: KEEPER re-put the refused credential, `Capabilities.put` set it back to `usable`, and REGROWS then found nothing to heal — so a relay that lost its `keys.redb` was never re-registered and its reads fell back to the write capability for ever, which is exactly mutant MC-14 and the inverse of what §13.3 item 7 promises. The rule is now decided in one place and in one transaction: while the pair has a **pending** row the credential is left refused and reads use the write capability (§21.2 point 8); otherwise the `done` rows go back to pending **and** the credential is re-put together with them. The re-arm and the re-put cannot be split, and the rule cannot live in two steps, for the same reason: `rejected` is a level and not an edge — it stays set until a new generation is installed — so a step that re-armed without re-putting would re-arm again at every later pass and would send the rows the work lane had just finished back to pending for ever. A `regrows()` triggered by a rejected credential (§7.2 lists it among REGROWS's triggers) still keeps the rows that must exist; KEEPER's pass is what re-arms the ones the relay lost |
| 16 | §5.4 "When", §7.2 | **Added by the review of S6 (2026-09-20): a missed week publishes at the next pass, and not at `t_pub(w)`.** The first version compared `now` with `t_pub(week(now))` before anything else, so an owner back online in week `w` with a bundle of `w − 2` — which is valid in no week (§5.4 "Validity") — published nothing for up to seven more days of being online, while every establishment, reset and reconnect fetch against `P_0` failed (§21.7). The due minute is now `t_pub` of the first week that was **not** published, which is `t_pub(w)` itself whenever nothing was missed, so §5.4's "A missed week publishes at the next time-triggered pass" holds without weakening PM-11 or PM-30: the value reads `dm_self.bundle_week` and the card's fixed offset and nothing else |
| 17 | §7.2 first sentence | **Added by the review of S6 (2026-09-20): KEEPER and REGROWS mint every credential and verifier before their transaction opens.** A `CRYPTO_UNAVAILABLE` returned from inside the transaction is a normal return, and a normal return **commits** — the `setRelays` and the credentials or rows the earlier loop iterations had already written — while reporting an error, which is not the all-or-nothing §7.2's first sentence states. The closed context is now found before any row is written, and the transaction holds database work only. The secrets are still zeroised in the frame that derived them |
| 18 | §13.6 step 5 | **Added by the review of S6 (2026-09-20): no "has this week a bundle op?" read exists.** `OwnerStore` carried one, unused, whose KDoc promised an **undecided** operation; `dm_outbound_op` has no outcome column, so the query could not tell an undecided operation from a decided or a failed one, and OWNER-WEEK wired to it would have refused a legitimate re-publication. It is gone, with a comment where it was: OWNER-WEEK's guard is `dm_self.bundle_week`, and S7's re-seal of a FAILED or INDETERMINATE bundle op reads `dm_self.bundle_published_week` (§21.8) |
