### 22.12 Forward notes (S7 implementation, 2026-09-21)

**Precedence.** These notes record what slice S7 (contacts from cards, the establishment state
machine, SEND and RECV, OUTCOME, requests, block and delete, the contact half of KEEPER, the DM
actor and the facade) delivered where the sections above say something else, and **what it
deliberately did not deliver**. They win over §0–§21 and over §22.0–§22.11 for the items they name,
and for nothing else. Items 11–16 are **deferrals**: they name work that is out of this slice's
scope or that S7 could not honestly finish, say exactly what is missing and what the code does
instead, and say which slice must add it. §22.11 items 8 and 9(a) are discharged here (the G-DM2
allowlist, `NoReflexTest`, the DM actor); §22.11 items 6 and 9(b, c) are not, and item 12 below
says so.

**Items 17–19 were added by the four-reviewer round of 2026-09-21**, after the rest of this table
was written. Items 17 and 18 record work the review showed was genuinely missing and that the
fixes then added; item 19 is a **new deferral** the review found and the fix could not close,
because closing it needs the closed JNI surface opened. Everything else the reviewers raised was
either fixed inside the slice's existing shape (no design consequence) or was not a defect; the
branch's commit messages carry that accounting.

| # | Section it corrects | What S7 does, and why |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | §2.6 closed list | **Two functions were added to the closed surface: 21 `provisionalKeys` and 22 `pairOffer`.** §2.6's list had no way to obtain either side of `K_prov(dir)` or to mint a pair root, and both are decided from material Kotlin must never hold. `provisionalKeys(h, contactId, sessionId, stateEnv, replySeedEnv, peerOlmIdentity)` derives the outgoing and incoming provisional keys **and their direction bytes** from the session's own `session_keys()` — the role is computed inside, by comparing the session's identity key with the context's, so no caller can present a wrong direction and no `STATE_CORRUPT` session of another pair can be used (the two unit tests are exactly those cases). `pairOffer(h, contactId, rootGen, baseWeek)` draws the root, wraps it and returns the offer to attach; a root drawn in Kotlin would be a secret §3.2 does not let it hold. Both are `Exported` on `CLOSED_LIST` with the slice name S7, and `dm-jni-surface.sh` was updated to match — the gate is what makes an addition deliberate, and this is the first slice to make one |
| 2 | §2.6 function 10 (`sealIntro`), function 12 (`openIntro`) | **Their argument lists differ from §2.6's, in both directions, and the reason is the same: the caller must not be able to disagree with the envelope.** `sealIntro` takes the **card index** rather than the sender's own card bytes and reads the PreKey base key out of the L1 it is given, instead of taking it as an argument, so binding v3 cannot be signed over a base key other than the one in the message (mutant PM-34 is about exactly that pair). `openIntro` re-opens the **outer** layer itself rather than taking the already-opened inner bytes: the peek order opens the outer layer to read `gen_id` and the inner chain index (§7.7), and handing those bytes back across the bridge would let a caller pair one envelope's outer layer with another's inner one. The cost is one repeated card-keyed AEAD per intro envelope that reaches RECV, which R-S5's budget carries (one decapsulation dominates it) |
| 3 | §2.6 functions 17 and 18 (`l0Encode`, `l0Decode`), R-B1 | **Both return the bucket, and Kotlin never computes one.** R-B1 makes the bucket a function of the body length alone, and mutants ME-22 and ME-3 are precisely a bucket computed a second way. `l0Encode` returns the chosen bucket beside the L0; `l0Decode` returns the bucket of the record it just decoded, which is what a **re-seal** needs — a pending send flushed at `EST-READY`, a FAILED item retried by the user, a resend of an INDETERMINATE op — because the original send's choice must be reproduced exactly. `DmBucketsTest` asserts that `:crypto-bridge` restates no bucket constant and that its one body cap equals `seal.rs`'s |
| 4 | §2.6 function 14 (`pairKeys`), §6.3 | **`pairKeys` takes the kind of key it is given: the root, or the week key `K_w`.** §6.3 lets the sender erase the root "as soon as `K_b` is computed" and the recipient erase its offer once a pair envelope opens under it; a `pairKeys` that could only start from the root would have made both erasures impossible, because every later week would need it again. With `PAIR_FROM_WEEK_KEY` the weekly ratchet `K_{w+1} = HKDF(K_w, …)` runs from the newest stored key, which is what `PairKeySteps` does time-triggered, and `PAIR_WEEK_WINDOW_MAX = 26` bounds one call |
| 5 | §13.1, §13.3 | **One `:sync` API addition: `Namespaces.listening(tx, namespace)`.** A bundle fetch has two states and `dm_contact` has one column for both — an open 15-minute attempt **window** and the wait of at least six hours before the next attempt, both in `fetch_listen_until_minute`, whose CHECK ties it to `establishment = 'fetching'`. The bit that tells them apart is `P_c`'s listening flag, which `:sync` already persists; a copy in `:messaging` would be lost at every restart and would turn a mid-window restart into either a lost attempt or an endless deferral. The precedent is `Capabilities.installed` in S6 (§22.11 item 3), and the reason is the same: a guard whose state already lives in `:sync` is read back from `:sync` |
| 6 | §13.8, §13.7 | **`DmClock` gained `monotonicMillis()`.** `ConsumerTail` runs to a **deadline**, and a deadline measured on the wall clock is one a clock change can extend or cancel. The wall-clock half (`nowEpochSeconds`) keeps deciding every minute, day and week the schema stores; the monotonic half decides only how long the actor keeps consuming |
| 7 | §21.7 point 2 | **`EST-START` registers `P_c` before it forgets its tombstones, not after.** §21.7 point 2 writes the forget first. `Inbox.forgetConsumed` refuses a namespace that is not registered at all, which `P_c` is not before the **first** establishment, so the stated order throws there. Inside one transaction the two are equivalent: the register reuses an existing row and keeps its tombstones, the forget then deletes them, and nothing can fetch in between — which is the whole point of the rule ("a bundle consumed during an earlier establishment must be fetchable again after a reset, a key change or a re-added contact") |
| 8 | §7.2 KEEPER row, §22.11 item 7 | **The contact half of KEEPER registers `X_c` itself, write-only.** A capability is scoped to a registered namespace, and KEEPER runs at a session start — before any send to that contact has registered anything. It therefore makes the same `Namespaces.register(listen = false, CapabilitySource.LOCAL)` that SEND's step S7 makes, with the same arguments, so whichever runs first the row is the same and the other is a no-op. **The re-put bound of §21.2 point 7 is "at most once per pass" here, not §22.11 item 15's registration rule:** a deposit credential is registered by its **owner**, not by the depositor, so this device has no `relay_key_registration` row to wait on; the bound is that a pass runs at a session start, a week change or a `CAPABILITIES` hint, and never on an inbound blob (T28) |
| 9 | §13.6, minute columns and `gen_id` | **Two unit conventions are fixed here, because both were wrong in the first implementation and neither shows up as anything but silence.** (a) Every `%_minute` column is the minute grid **in epoch seconds** (`x % 60 = 0`), and `peer_bind_minute` is compared with `author_minute` in `SessionSlots`, so the wire's minute **index** is multiplied once, where the intro envelope is opened, and the parameter is named `bindingSeconds` from there on. Storing the index made every R-SS1 comparison 60 times too small and failed the CHECK outright. (b) `gen_id` is a `u32` and crosses the bridge as a signed `Int`, so `ContactStore.unsigned` widens it wherever it is bound to `dm_peer_pq`, `olm_session.peer_gen_id` or `olm_prekey_used` — `Int.toLong()` failed the `BETWEEN 0 AND 4294967295` CHECK for **half** the generations a peer can publish, which no fixed-seed test would ever hit. `PeekOrder` and `ReceiveStep` already read it that way; now every path does |
| 10 | §15.1, §16.1 | **`DmWorld` and `NoReflexTest`: what they prove and what they do not.** `DmWorld` gives each device its own migrated v5 database and its own real `ghost-dm-host` context, and moves enqueued blobs into whichever inbox listens on their namespace **at that moment** — the envelope path, not the Phase 7 engine. The outbox's lanes, leases, quorum and outcome machinery stay Phase 7's, proved by `sync-exit-gate`; the `DmWorld` of §15.1 that drives the engine against a `ModelRelay` is S10's. `NoReflexTest` is §16.1's behavioural half of T28: two worlds run the same script on the same clock, delivery is split by recipient so A's establishment is identical in both, and the only difference is whether B's inbox received anything. It compares `relayVisible()`, which is deliberately **wider than the wire** (namespace rows, capability rows and registration rows, not only stores), because T28 forbids the local decision too. **One difference is declared rather than asserted away:** accepting a request makes the contact sendable, so the next KEEPER pass installs its deposit credential and registers `X_c` — local rows, no store and no registration on the wire, since a deposit credential is registered by its owner. The test pins the stores as equal and the local rows as different, so the day an accept enqueues, the first assertion fails |
| 11 | §8.3, §5.5, §8.6 | **Discharged by S8 (§22.13).** The original note, kept as the record of what S7 left: **Deferred to S8: there is no key change, no `dm_held` and no restore.** An intro envelope whose bound Olm identity differs from the pinned one is **dropped and counted** (`untrusted`, or `request_key_mismatch` for a request, which also deletes the request row per R-P2b); a verified bundle that names another Olm identity makes `EST-READY` drop the candidate and set `reset_needed`, and **no session is built**. Refusing is the safe half of §8.3 — silently pinning the new identity is mutant PM-16 — but it is not the rule: the rule holds the ciphertext undecrypted until the user decides. **What S8 must add:** the classification, `dm_held` with `hold_until_minute`, the KEYDEC transaction and the HELD replay of §22.8.1, plus `verifyKeyChange`/`acknowledgeKeyChange` and the restore path of §8.6 |
| 12 | §22.11 item 6, §5.3 | **Still deferred: the off-schedule bundle rank is still 1, not `1 + max(rank)`.** §22.11 item 6 assigned this to S7 because `BUNDLE` would exist. It does, but it reads a **contact's** `P_c`; reading the owner's own `P_0` needs a fetch credential for `P_0`, a listening window on it and a claim path for own bundles, none of which any step of this slice has. The consequence is unchanged and bounded by the week: two off-schedule publications in one week both rank 1, and fetchers break the tie by the smaller blob hash instead of by recency. **What the next slice that touches ACTIVATE must add** is unchanged too: read the candidates of `P_0` before the ACTIVATE transaction and pass `1 + max(rank)` into `publishBundle`, whose `rank` argument is already the seam |
| 13 | §10, §21.12 | **Deferred to S9: ephemeral timers beyond the row's own lifecycle, SWEEP and erasure.** S7 writes `delete_at_minute`, `unread_deadline_minute` and `seen_until_day` at insert and every registered query excludes expired rows (`DmDaoQueries`), so no expired row is ever displayed; the countdown, the DISPLAY transaction, the notification path, the SWEEP cascade and the erasure half of §21.3 point 5 are S9's. `PairKeySteps` implements the **derivation** half only, and says so in its own header: without it a conversation crossing a week boundary loses both pair keys and falls to `NO_SEAL_KEY` and a relay-visible re-establishment, which is a regression §6.8 rule 4 calls a bug — the erasure that makes P-7 and T34 true (and that mutant MC-21 is about) waits for SWEEP. `ContactSteps` records orphaned namespaces in `dm_orphan_namespace` and removes nothing, for the same reason. **One smaller gap inside this:** the pending-send flush re-seals a stored L0 with the bucket `l0Decode` returns, which is the bucket the original send chose; a `TIMER_SET` that **shortened** the conversation's timer between the send and the flush therefore still travels in the earlier TTL bucket. It is inside §10's declared bounds (the relay TTL is a bucket, not the timer) and costs at most one message's extra relay lifetime |
| 14 | §15.6, Q37, V-13 | **Deferred: the hint benchmark was not run.** Q37's bound and V-13's measurement are about how many recognition keys one peek pass may try before R-S5's foreground budget is at risk. `RecognitionKeys` implements the rule that matters for correctness — at most one AEAD attempt per blob (R-S5, mutant PM-18), candidates in MRU contact order, provisional keys cached per session — and the flow worlds exercise it, but nothing measures it. The task's own priority was the establishment state machine and OUTCOME idempotency over the benchmark, and that is what the time went to. **What the measuring slice must add:** the benchmark with a contact count that makes the bound meaningful, on the reference device, beside the §15.10 step 9 timings |
| 15 | §7.6 D2b, §12.3 | **What the rejected-request case actually does, recorded because it reads like a bug.** A rejected request deletes the contact, its session and its rows; the reuse tuple `olm_prekey_used(gen_id, session_id)` survives, because it is keyed by the generation and cascades only when that generation is erased. A's next message to B before her session is acknowledged is another PreKey message of the **same** Olm session, so B refuses it at D2b (`drop(replay)`) and no second request appears — and the gap hold delays even that, since it is chain index 1 of a chain B no longer has. This is §7.6 read literally ("a replayed first flight after its session was replaced or deleted") and it is consistent with §12.3's "the requester cannot tell whether it was seen, accepted or rejected"; A recovers when `UNACK_MAX` re-establishes with a new session id (§8.7). `DmFlowTest.aRejectedRequestLeavesNoContact` pins it so that a later slice changes it on purpose or not at all |
| 16 | §16.1, §17.1 S7 row | **What of the S7 row is not in this branch.** The exit-gate `DmWorld` suites of §15.1 and the privacy worlds NI-DM1 … NI-DM7 are S10's and S11's and are not started here; `messaging-no-network.sh` (G-DM1) and the widened scopes of §22.11 item 9(c) are still the S0′ gate slice's, unchanged; the Keystore wrappers of §22.11 item 9(b) are still S12's. What this slice did add to the gates is the G-DM2 allowlist: `SendStep.kt`, `EstablishmentSteps.kt` and `ContactKeeper.kt` with the transactions they hold, and `DmActor.kt` on the trigger list — every one of them named deliberately, because the list refuses a file it does not know |
| 17 | §21.7 point 7, §16.1, gate G-DM2 | **`BUNDLE` is its own file, `BundleStep.kt`, and the gate's allowlist is what made that necessary.** G-DM2 allows the restricted calls **by file**, on the stated argument that "a Kotlin file holds one step"; `EstablishmentSteps.kt` held four, and `bundle` is the one establishment transaction driven directly by an arriving blob — the forbidden side of T28. Allowlisting the file for `EST-START`, `EST-READY` and `EST-END` therefore granted BUNDLE the same permission silently, and nothing but inspection kept it from using it. Splitting the file restores the invariant the gate depends on and puts §21.7 point 7's "RECV, BUNDLE and HELD never call `Outbox.enqueue`, `Namespaces.*`, `Registrations` or `Capabilities.put`" back under the gate. The candidate layout, its validity rule and its deterministic nonce moved with it, into `BundleCandidates`, because `EST-READY` reads back what `BUNDLE` wrote. Two smaller corrections of the same gate: the scope functions `also` and `let` were listed as covered while contributing **zero** coverage (they bind the port to `it`, never to `this`, so they can only produce a call the bare-call branch excludes), and they now feed the alias list instead; and comment lines are stripped before matching, because a KDoc sentence containing `request (` was a reportable "call site" in any file that scoped over a port |
| 18 | §12.4, §21.12 point 3, §13.5, §8.2 R-SS0 | **Four rules that were stated in the design and absent from the code, found by the review and added.** (a) **`block` and `deleteContact` never stopped listening on `P_c`.** Both leave the contact where the actor no longer visits it (`card_only`, or deleted), so no `EST-END` could ever close a window a block interrupted: the device kept `list`/`get` on a blocked contact's bundle namespace with its READ credentials installed, which is §12.4's "relay-visible: nothing" broken and L7-D6's "at most three session starts per establishment" unbounded (PM-31). The close is `EstablishmentSteps.abandonFetch`, called from `ContactSteps` — the call has to sit in an allowlisted file, and closing a fetch window is `EST-END`'s own first line. `Namespaces.remove` stays SWEEP's (§21.12 point 3). (b) **`recordOrphans` could silently do nothing and the delete committed anyway**, leaving `X_c` and `P_c` with `:sync` rows nothing on the device could name again, since the card they derive from had just been erased — the ≤ 7 d of L7-D7 has no meaning without that row. It is now fail-closed: no orphan row, no block and no delete. The context is resolved **before** the transaction, because `SelfContexts.get()` opens one of its own when its cache is cold and a nested transaction is an exception out of the facade. (c) **`block` deleted `dm_pending_send` without deciding the item**, leaving a `dm_outbound_item` `pending` with its sealed L0 and a `dm_message` `queued` for ever (with the timer off neither expires); both now move to `failed`, and `dm_held` and `dm_scheduled` — in §12.4's list and in §7.2's KEYDEC row — are cleared too, inert until S8 writes them. `ContactStore.block` also clears `pending_identity`, without which "block of a changed contact" (§7.2's own KEYDEC case) would abort on the `(trust_state = 'changed') = (pending_identity IS NOT NULL)` CHECK, which §21.4 rule 5 says is always a bug. (d) **R-SS0 and R-SS2 were dead code.** `EST-READY` read "a session row exists" where §21.7 point 4 says "a **usable** session already exists", so a send on a session 28 days unanswered (§8.7) re-established, fetched a candidate, threw it away, reset `fetch_attempts` and left the dead session in `current` — for ever, three session starts per user send. `UNACK_MAX` and `NO_SEAL_KEY` now demote `current` where the re-establishment is decided (R-SS0), and a message that decrypts on `previous` runs `SessionSlots.swapAfterPreviousDecrypted` (R-SS2), without which two devices never converge on one session. §13.5's `unreachable → fetching (user retry)` edge exists now too, and the pending items of a contact the establishment loop no longer visits are retried by the time-triggered pass — §21.7 point 4's "`StorageFullException` → retried at the next time-triggered pass", which nothing did |
| 19 | §21.8 point 5 | **Deferred: a FAILED or INDETERMINATE bundle publication is still not re-sealed, and `dm_self.bundle_published_week` still has no reader.** OUTCOME writes the column where §21.8 point 5 says to write it; the step that should read it — "OWNER-WEEK … re-seals the current bundle (new salt, same generation and `bundle_rank`) when `bundle_published_week < bundle_week` and `now < start(bundle_week + 2) − 1 h`" — cannot be written in `:messaging` at all. The closed surface of §2.6 has exactly one bundle function, `bundleCreate`, and it draws a **fresh** generation, so the same generation cannot be re-sealed. The obvious alternative — re-depositing the retained bytes of the failed op, as §7.5's RESEND does for an item — does not substitute for it either: Phase 7 allows identical bytes in a namespace only once the earlier op is **released and its ciphertext wiped** (`OutboxStore.enqueue`, "these bytes are already queued under another operation"), and that is a state of `outbox_op` which `:messaging` cannot observe — `dm_outbound_op.blob` says only that *this* layer still holds the bytes, not that the outbox has finished with them. An OWNER-WEEK pass deciding from `bundle_published_week` alone therefore throws on a publication that is merely still in flight, which is how this was found. The *new salt* of the rule is what makes the re-publication expressible at all, and it is load-bearing rather than cosmetic. **The consequence, undeclared until now:** a publication that ends FAILED costs the owner that whole week's bundle, contacts fetching `P_0` find nothing, and every establishment against this device fails until next week's `t_pub`. **What the next slice that opens the JNI surface must add:** a closed-list `bundleReseal(h, cardIndex, week, rank, genId, secretsEnv)` that re-seals the stored generation's public halves under a fresh salt, `dm-jni-surface.sh` updated to match, and the OWNER-WEEK branch above, with an OS case for a FAILED and an INDETERMINATE publication. **Still open after S8, which did open the surface (§22.13 item 1): see §22.13 item 12 for why it was not taken there** |

### 22.13 Forward notes (S8 implementation, 2026-09-21)

**Precedence.** These notes record what slice S8 (key-change classification and hold, KEYDEC and
HELD, safety numbers, revocation v2 and the scheduled notices, reset and staleness, restore and
`reconnect`) delivered where the sections above say something else, and **what it deliberately did
not deliver**. They win over §0–§21 and over §22.0–§22.12 for the items they name, and for nothing
else. §22.12 item 11 is discharged here in full, and §22.11 item 11's "S8 owns it" with it. Items
9–14 are **deferrals**: they name work that is out of this slice's scope or that S8 could not
honestly finish, say exactly what is missing and what the code does instead, and say which slice
must add it.

Items 3a and 15–20 were added by the **review of this slice** (four adversarial reviewers,
2026-09-21). Three of them correct a rule an earlier item stated and the code did not keep (3a, 8,
17); three record a behaviour the code now has that no section above describes (15, 16, 19); and
one declares a consequence of a standing deferral that was never written down (18). Item 20 records
what the review showed is still untested.

| # | Section it corrects | What S8 does, and why |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | §2.6 closed list, §22.9 item 12 | **Three functions were added to the closed surface: 23 `revocationIssue`, 23 `revocationVerify` and 24 `l1Parse`.** §8.5 puts the revocation certificate v2 among the four X-21 artifacts "signed and verified in Rust", and §2.6's table — written before §22 re-sourced X-21 — names no function for either half; `l0Encode` and `l0Decode` carry the 102 bytes as an opaque TLV value and that is all they do with them. Issuing needs the `ghost1` seed, which is one of the four secrets the context holds and which §3.2 does not let Kotlin hold, and it derives the identity key **from that seed** rather than taking it as an argument, so a certificate can only ever name the identity that signed it; verifying needs `verify_strict`, which S0′ V-14 showed BouncyCastle cannot reproduce, so a Kotlin verifier would accept certificates the crate refuses. **24 `l1Parse` is §2.6's own `L1.parse`**, which §22.9 item 12 deferred on the argument that `openOuter` and `openIntro` return the header beside the L1 they just opened. That held for every S7 caller and stops holding at HELD: a `dm_held` row stores the L1 and **not** the envelope (which is why `gen_id` and `prov_env` are separate columns), and §22.8.1 rule 1 has the replay group the rows by `session_id` and order each group by the inner chain index before the first `sessionInbound` runs. All three are `Exported` on `CLOSED_LIST`, `dm-jni-surface.sh` matches, and `DmField` gains `IssuedMinute = 0x3c` and `RevocationReason = 0x3d` on both sides |
| 2 | §13.2 `issueRevocation` | **`issueRevocation(reason)` returns the 102 certificate bytes; it does not return `Unit`, and it stores nothing.** Schema v5 has **no column for a self-issued certificate**: `dm_scheduled.payload` hangs off a contact and `dm_self` has none, and §13.6's step 5 is closed. §8.5 separates issuing ("local") from propagation ("the explicit user action `notifyContactsOfRevocation()`"), so something has to hold the bytes between the two taps, and the choice is between reopening migration 5 for one row and handing the caller a value. The bytes are **public and signed**, and every contact that receives one keeps a copy for good, so holding them in the Phase 13 UI leaks nothing a recipient's copy does not. `notifyContactsOfRevocation(certificate)` takes them back and verifies them under this identity's own key before scheduling anything |
| 3 | §22.8.4 R-SS1, §13.6 step 5 | **HELD supplies `consume_minute` where `RECV` supplies `binding_minute`, and this is the one place the session-age input is not the contact's clock.** R-SS1 compares an intro envelope's `binding_minute` with a stored `peer_bind_minute`, both written by the contact; `dm_held` carries **no** binding-minute column and migration 5 is closed. It is sound where it is used: a KEYDEC acceptance deletes **both** sessions (R-SS0) before HELD runs, so every comparison the replay can make is between two held rows of one `changed` window, and their arrival order at this device is the only order either side agrees on — it is also the order §22.8.1 rule 1 already sorts the groups by. The value is on the minute grid in epoch seconds, like every other minute column (§22.12 item 9a) |
| 3a | item 3, §22.8.1 rule 1, §22.8.4 rule 2 | **The consume minute HELD supplies to R-SS1 is the *group's* smallest, not the row's own** — a correction to item 3, which asserted an invariant the first implementation did not keep. Item 3's whole argument is that the substitution is sound *because* `consume_minute` "is also the order §22.8.1 rule 1 already sorts the groups by"; that holds only if the number fed to `admitNewInbound` is the number the groups were sorted by. It was not: groups were ordered by their smallest `consume_minute` while each row carried its own, and the row that reaches `admitNewInbound` is the one with the lowest **chain index** — which §7.7's gap hold explicitly lets arrive after a later index, once `HOLD_MAX` passes elapse. Presented out of order, §22.8.4 rule 2's `drop(stale_session)` fires on a group whose chain-first row is younger than the previous group's, `markFirstFlightUsed` never runs, so every remaining row of that session takes the same path, and HELD deletes each one as it drops: **every message of that session lost, silently, inside the decision the user took to release them**. Each row now carries its group's minute, so the sort key and R-SS1's input are one number. The row's own `consume_minute` still drives everything item 4 lists (R9's freshness, R-O1's `sort_minute`, C-11's unread cap), which are per-row by nature |
| 4 | §21.10 points 3 and 5, §7.7 R-O1 | **The replay measures three things from the held row's own `consume_minute`, not from `now`**: R9's freshness (§21.10 point 3 says so), R-O1's `sort_minute`, and C-11's unread cap. Without the first, a user who takes three weeks over a key change is answered with a message the very decision that released it refuses as stale; without the second, a released hold sorts weeks away from the burst it belonged to. The cap is the third: §21.10 point 5's "a replayed message whose computed unread deadline is ≤ `now_e` is not inserted (counted `expired_while_held`)" is implemented in `insertIncoming`, so the row is **never written** and the T25 bound needs nothing extra |
| 5 | §8.3, §8.2 rule 4, §7.4 R6 | **A first flight under the *old* pinned identity, while the change is undecided, is dropped and counted.** §7.4's R6 names two `changed` cases — a PreKey message under the pending key is held, a normal message continues under the pinned sessions — and a first flight under the **old** key is neither. §8.2 rule 4 is the rule that decides it ("no session row is created for a contact in `changed`"), and letting it through would build a third session for a contact the user is at that moment being asked about. Holding it instead would buy nothing: a held row is replayed only under the identity the user accepted, and this one is the other. `KeyChangeTest.aFirstFlightUnderTheOldIdentityIsRefusedWhileTheChangeIsUndecided` pins it |
| 6 | §6.3 "Replacement", `ROOT_REPLACE_WEEKS` | **Trigger (c) is implemented; (a) and (b) hold by construction and are now asserted.** A restored device has no `dm_pair_offer` rows at all and a KEYDEC acceptance erases the secrets of the ones it has, so in both cases the planning read finds no live offer and mints `1 + max(root_gen)` (§21.3 point 3). (c) needs a clock: `week(now) ≥ base_week + ROOT_REPLACE_WEEKS` for the **newest** root, acknowledged or not, and without it a root acknowledged once was never replaced — a seizure of either device would read every pair envelope of that conversation back to its base week, which is the window the bound exists to close. `ROOT_REPLACE_WEEKS = 13`, §6.3's value for a client without PQ-C; S13 lowers it to 4 |
| 7 | §22.11 item 11, §22.12 item 11, §21.12 point 2 | **ACTIVATE recognises a `dm_self` row of another identity, and a restore onto one clears the DM tables instead of reporting `ALREADY_ACTIVE`.** §22.11 item 11 left the choice between wiping `dm_self` with the identity and making ACTIVATE recognise a foreign row; the second is what the schema admits, because `dm_self` carries no identity column and nothing in it could (`seen_key`, `content_key_wrapped` and `state_key_wrapped` are random). What **does** name the identity is `dm_own_namespace`: `X_0` and `P_0` are HKDF outputs of `card_secret_0`. A row set that does not match the namespaces this context derives is somebody else's, and a restore onto it would otherwise set no `accept_after_minute`, publish no bundle and leave the restored identity mute. The two stale namespaces become `dm_orphan_namespace` rows **before** anything is erased, the same fail-closed order §12.4's block uses, and SWEEP removes them (§21.12 point 3). An **empty** namespace set counts as matching, so a half-written activation resumed by §21.12 point 2 is completed rather than wiped. `RestoreScan.restore` refuses while `identity.hasIdentity()` and nothing in the tree removes an identity while keeping the DM tables, so this is **armed rather than exercised** — it is written because the alternative is a silent `UNCHANGED` nobody would see |
| 8 | §22.10 item 3, `DmExpiredRowLint` | **`dm_held` and `dm_scheduled` do not join `expiringTables`, and the reason is the lint's own scope rule.** §22.10 item 3 says the expiring table is `dm_message` and the column is `delete_at_minute`, and that the other rows SWEEP deletes by time "reach no view, so their bound is the sweep itself; if S7 gives one of them a read path a user sees, it joins `expiringTables` then". Neither of these does: the HELD replay and the due-control step are engine steps, not read paths, and nothing they return reaches the facade. Each carries the bound it needs in its own query instead — `heldFor` takes `nowMinute` and leaves a row past `hold_until_minute` alone, `due` takes it and returns nothing early — so a row past its deadline is never replayed and never sent, and deleting it is SWEEP's (S9). The lint's behavioural half, which compares two worlds differing only in expired rows, has no meaning for a step that is not a read path. **Corrected by the review of this slice:** that sentence was false for a *third* read. The recovery predicate of §22.8.1 ("live held rows on a contact that is no longer `changed`") was written as a raw query in `DmActor` rather than in the DAO, and it carried no `hold_until_minute` bound — so a contact whose key change was decided **after** some of its rows had expired was selected by every pass, hint-triggered ones included, for ever: two SQLite transactions to replay nothing, until SWEEP ships in S9. That is exactly the shape the lint's static half catches, a filter present in one read of a table and absent in another, and it could not, because `dm_held` is not in `expiringTables`. The query moved into `HeldStore.contactsWithReleasedHolds` with the bound, and the `DELETE FROM dm_held`/`dm_scheduled` statements written inline in `ReceiveStep` and `ContactSteps` moved there too: **keeping every read and write of these two tables in one file is what stands in for the lint here**, and is the rule a later slice must keep rather than re-litigate. `heldCount` and `oldestHeld` stay unbounded on purpose — they mirror the `dm_held_limit` trigger, which counts unfiltered |

The bound is what cannot be written: `dm_scheduled` has no attempt column and v5 is closed, so a rescheduling step either counts nothing and loops for ever or keeps the counter in memory, where a process start resets it — and an unbounded loop of control sends is a relay-visible behaviour worse than the loss it would repair. **What the code does instead:** the item ends `failed` like any other, its L0 is kept until its deadline, and the user's next action — another `reconnect(contacts)`, another `notifyContactsOfRevocation` — schedules a fresh one, which is what a user who notices the contact never answered would do anyway. **What the slice that adds it must add first:** an attempt column on `dm_scheduled` (or an `attempts` column on `dm_outbound_item`), then the OUTCOME branch and the `control_failed` counter. The bound on the exposure is the hold deadline itself — ≤ 30 d + 1 h from the consume minute, and the generation retention is 91 days past supersession — so the case needs a hold that outlives a generation by two months. **What S9 must add:** the generation sweep's `WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dm_held WHERE gen_id = …)`, and an EP case that holds a message across a generation erasure |

A device back after the whole spread found every row due and sent them all in one relay session — k deposits into k inboxes, the degree-k leak of RP §3.8 the offsets were drawn to prevent, arriving by a different road. `runDue` now takes `LIMIT 1` and sends **one** control per pass across every contact; a backlog of k drains over k time-triggered passes (the actor timer, a database open, a relay session start) instead of inside the drawn spread, which is the right trade because the spread exists for the degree and not for the latency. **And a transient failure no longer loses the notice.** The row was deleted whatever the send answered, including `NO_REPLICAS` (one week's slot table momentarily short of quorum), `STORAGE_FULL` and `CRYPTO_UNAVAILABLE` — so "I lost my phone" could end with a contact that never learns, silently and with no counter, which is not what the code comment justified (a contact *blocked* between the action and the minute) and not what item 9 defers (a **FAILED** control, post-enqueue, which is OUTCOME's and still deferred). Those three outcomes now re-draw a fresh offset from the notice's own spread and persist it, which keeps the privacy property of the first draw and keeps a contact this device cannot reach from holding the head of the queue. A permanent refusal still deletes. **The row is also retired inside the send's own transaction** (`SendStep.sendControl(scheduledId = …)`), the same correction the S7 review made to RESEND (commit 8bd2f0a): the delete-after-send it replaced meant a crash in between deposited the notice **twice**, and neither dedup catches that — `sendControl` mints a fresh `message_id`, so D3's `seen_tag` differs, and it is a fresh ratchet step, so D2b differs too. The comment that claimed otherwise was wrong on both halves |

Nothing deleted the `WHAT_RECONNECT` row on a user send, so a user who messaged a re-added contact inside the 48-hour window got the renewal their own message caused **and** a redundant `session_reset` deposit afterwards — relay-visible traffic a normative sentence says will not happen, and not among item 9's declared deferrals. `SendStep.commit` now deletes the contact's reconnect row in the user send's own transaction, and only for a user send: a CONTROL is not one, so the reconnect notice does not cancel itself and `resetSession`'s `reset` control does not cancel it either |

The path with no second tap is the one that matters: `Messaging.onRestored()` runs from the K20 hook exactly once, and `activate(restored = true)` returns `NO_RELAYS` **without writing anything** when `esSlots.slotsInWeek(week)` is empty — the normal state of a fresh install a second after a restore, before the relay directory and the ES schedule facts have synced. The actor now runs the resume when the context is missing, **on a time-triggered pass only** (T28: ACTIVATE publishes a bundle, so an arriving blob must not be able to cause one; all three of §21.12 point 2's triggers are time-triggered anyway). It runs it with `restored = true` and not with the caller's flag, because the flag died with the call that failed and `true` is the side that is safe in both cases — a restored identity keeps its `accept_after_minute`, and an identity that was not restored cannot lose anything, since without a `dm_self` row it has published no bundle, registered no namespace and could not have been written to |
| 18 | §8.6 healing path (b), items 12–13, §22.12 item 12 | **Declared: the off-schedule rank deferral can cost a restored device up to a week of one-way establishment failures.** Item 13 keeps the rank at 1 rather than `1 + max(rank)` and describes the consequence as "ordered by the smaller blob hash instead of by recency". For §8.6's healing path (b) that description is too mild and the consequence was written down nowhere: the path *depends* on the restored bundle "outrank[ing] the lost device's bundles of the same week by `bundle_rank`", and if the lost device also published off-schedule at rank 1 in weeks `w−1 … w+1` — its own ACTIVATE, or an earlier restore — both bundles tie at `(w, 1)` and §5.3's tie-break is the smaller blob hash, a coin flip. A contact that loses the toss establishes against the **lost** device's generation, and every such session produces `unknown_generation` at the restored device until the next week's OWNER-WEEK bundle wins by week: up to seven days of silent, one-way failure, healed only by the contact resetting again or by the user's own send. The bound is the week, the residue is declared here, and the fix is item 12's `1 + max(rank)`, which needs a read of the owner's own `P_0` |
| 19 | §8.5, §7.4 R5, §8.2 rule 4 | **HELD re-reads trust inside every row's transaction, and stops when it leaves `unverified`/`verified`.** `RECV` drops a blocked or revoked contact before any session call (R5) and the replay owed the same guard: the list the loop walks is read **before** the first transaction, and a `revocation` TLV carried by an earlier held row revokes the contact and deletes its held rows in that row's transaction while the loop still holds the rest in memory. The loop then ran `sessionInbound` for the next group and inserted an `olm_session` row for a **revoked** contact — which §8.2 rule 4 forbids — and inserted its messages, which §8.5's "nothing shown from B" forbids. Rows of the *same* session were saved only by accident (the reuse tuple the revocation row wrote made them `drop(replay)`), so the hole was exactly "a revocation in an earlier group, further groups behind it". The same guard covers a contact that returned to `changed` under a third key mid-pass, which the per-row pin check caught only by coincidence. Two other stop conditions joined it, for the same reason — a deletion must not destroy the only copy of a ciphertext for a reason that is not the row's: the contact row disappearing under the loop, and `L1.parse` answering `Unavailable`. **`L1.parse` gained that answer** (`DmCode.CRYPTO_UNAVAILABLE` and a missing library both mapped to `Invalid`, which HELD treats as permanent and **deletes**); every other typed facade of §2.6 already separated the two. The parse-failure counters moved inside the transaction that deletes the row, so a pass that stops early cannot count the same row twice |
| 20 | item 14, §22.8.1 rule 4 | **What the S8 review showed is still untested, and stays S10's.** Rule 4 names three KC3b halves; `KeyChangeTest.severalHeldMessagesOfOneSessionReplayInChainOrder` covers one (three rows of one session, in order). The other two — more than 40 held rows of one session inserted in random `held_id` order, and a RECV-created session before HELD runs — have no test on this branch, and **neither has the multi-group case of item 3a**, which is what would have caught that defect: three sessions held at once, with at least one group whose chain-first row arrived after a later chain index, asserted to lose no message. Arranging it needs a world that can drive the §7.7 gap hold to release a later chain index before index 0 arrives, which is `DmWorld` harness work the KC suite owns. Two gaps the review named **were** closed. `UNACK_MAX = 28` is now pinned on **both** sides by `TrustFlowTest` (a send on day 27 reuses the session; a send on day 29 demotes it and re-establishes), which it was not: the two existing tests advanced 35 and 29 days and asserted only the positive side, so a mutant `UNACK_MAX_DAYS = 1` survived both, because with a smaller bound nothing time-triggered replaces the session either and the later send still re-establishes. And `NoReflexTest` now covers the HELD **recovery** trigger — `DmActor.replayReleasedHolds`, which sits outside the `if (timeTriggered)` block and therefore runs on a pass an arriving blob caused. Its two S8 cases both replayed *inside* the facade call that decided, where the trigger is the user; the new one takes the KEYDEC transaction alone, the way a crash between the decision's commit and the replay leaves it, and drives a hint pass |

---

### 22.14 Forward notes (S9 implementation, 2026-09-22)

**Precedence.** These notes record what slice S9 (ephemeral: timer codes and versions, TTL and
deadline mapping, the countdowns, the SWEEP cascade and its checkpoint, the alarm and its
receiver, the notification and media ports, and the T25 tests) delivered where the sections above
say something else, and **what it deliberately did not deliver**. They win over §0–§21 and over
§22.0–§22.13 for the items they name, and for nothing else. §22.12 item 13 is discharged here in
full — the erasure half of the pair keys, the orphan namespaces and the countdowns it deferred —
and so is §22.13 item 10. Items 10–14 are **deferrals**: they name work that is out of this
slice's scope or that S9 could not honestly finish, say exactly what is missing and what the code
does instead, and say which slice must add it. Items **15 and 16** are the **corrections the
slice's own adversarial review made to it**, kept as their own rows rather than folded into the
items they correct: several of them were rules this section had already claimed to keep, and a
correction that edits the claim away leaves nothing to learn from.

| # | Section it corrects | What S9 does, and why |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | §10.6, §7.2 SWEEP row, §21.3 point 5 | **The order of the cascade is a dependency order, not the order §10.6 lists.** Held rows go **first**, before the messages: §5.4's "a generation referenced by a live held row is not erased before that row's `hold_until_minute`" and §22.13 item 10's `NOT EXISTS` mean that a generation whose last holder expires *in this pass* must be erasable *in this pass*, and it is only if the hold has already gone when the generation query runs. Then the messages (with `MediaPort.onMessagesDeleted(tx, ids)` inside the same transaction), the expired requests, the pending sends past `fail_after_hour`, `dm_seen`, the pair keys and offered roots, the generations, the retired sessions and `prov_env`, the stale `dm_peer_pq` rows, the orphan namespaces, the skipped-key prune, and last `expiry_clock_minute`. The clock is raised **last** so that a transaction that rolls back leaves it where it was: R-T7's column is the one piece of state a partial sweep must not advance. Two further things about the transaction, both found by this slice's own review. **The 500 bounds the transaction, not the sweep:** `sweep()` repeats it while a batch came back full, up to 10 000 rows, because the leftovers of a full batch have deadlines in the **past** while every "next wake" query is strictly in the future, so nothing would have been scheduled to come back for them. And **`SQLITE_FULL` does not escape SWEEP:** it is the first step of every pass (§13.7's order), so a full disk propagating out of it would take `RECV`, `OUTCOME` and the bundle claims with it — a device that cannot delete would also stop receiving. The catch is typed and narrow (§13.1, §21.12 point 1); a CHECK violation or a trigger abort still propagates |
| 2 | §21.3 point 5, §21.9 rule 1, §22.12 item 13 | **The pair-key ratchet moved out of the actor's time-triggered branch and into SWEEP**, where §21.3 point 5 puts it ("SWEEP first derives and stores … then erases … **in one transaction**"). S7 ran it as a separate time-triggered step because SWEEP did not exist, and the safe placement for a step with no owner is the restricted one; it said so in its own header. The move makes it run on a **hint** pass too, which §21.9 rule 2 admits by name ("Hint- and facade-triggered passes run only SWEEP, RECV, BUNDLE, …") and which the reason behind T28 admits as well: the ratchet writes `dm_pair_key` rows and nothing else — no store, no registration, no capability, no namespace change — so no relay can see that it happened. `PairKeySteps` now holds both halves and no longer opens a transaction of its own; mutant **MC-21** is the order of the two calls inside `sweep` |
| 3 | §6.3, §21.3 point 3, §22.8.5 rule 1 | **What the erasure half erases, and what it keeps.** In keys go at `start(w + 1) + FRESH` with `FRESH = 45 d` — not `FRESH(bucket)`, because a week key serves every message of its week whatever that message's timer, so it takes the longest of them (§21.10 point 3 keeps 45 d for the pair-key window explicitly). Out keys go except for `K_{week(now)}` of the newest generation and the weeks a **live retryable item** could still be re-encrypted under. The author week of such an item is not a column: `dm_outbound_item` carries `deadline_hour`, and a CONTROL or a REACTION has no `dm_message` row of its own to read an author minute from. The week is therefore derived from the deadline — `author ∈ [deadline_hour − 7 d − 1 h, deadline_hour]`, so the author week is one of at most three — and that whole interval is kept. It is a **superset** of the rule and costs nothing against P-7: the item is gone at its deadline, so the extra weeks are erased at the same instant the exactly-derived one would have been. An offered root's secret goes at `start(base_week + 1) + FRESH`, and the row itself only once a higher generation exists, which is §21.3 point 3's own condition |
| 4 | §6.3 "Replacement", §22.13 item 6 | **§6.3 gives an offered root two clocks, and the earlier one is not rule (c).** The secret is erased "when a pair envelope under its generation opens (acknowledged) **or at `start(b + 1) + FRESH`, whichever comes first** (a new root is then offered on my next user message)" — about 7.4 weeks — while replacement rule (c) fires at `ROOT_REPLACE_WEEKS = 13`. Until this slice nothing erased the secret, so (c) was the only rule that could fire, and S8's `aRootOlderThanTheBoundIsReplacedAtTheNextSend` advanced 12 weeks to show that it had not yet; with the erasure in place, 12 weeks is past the earlier bound and the test would have measured that instead. It now stays inside both windows and then crosses both. **The consequence, declared:** rule (c) is subsumed for an unacknowledged root and has no case of its own that this world can reach — it becomes the earlier of the two, and load-bearing again, exactly when S13 lowers `ROOT_REPLACE_WEEKS` to 4 (§9.4, Q49). The code for (c) stays where S8 put it |
| 5 | §2.6 function 20, §7.7 point 4 | **`DmEnvelopes` gains `prune`, and SWEEP calls it on every live session.** `DmSessions.prune` has existed on the bridge since S4b and had no caller; §21.3 point 6 as S0′ corrected it (V-15, Q67) makes SWEEP the caller, and mutant **MC-30** is this call not being made. A session whose state does not re-wrap (`StateCorrupt`) is **left alone rather than deleted**: that is a typed outcome of §2.1 rule 2, and a sweep is not the place to decide a session is unusable — the next `decrypt` answers the same code, and `UNACK_MAX` or the user's reset resolves it. `Unavailable` stops the loop, because there is nothing to prune with |
| 6 | §13.2, §13.1 | **Three ports and two facade additions, with their deviations from §13.2 named.** `markDisplayed(ids)` returns an `Int` where §13.2 shows no return: it is a **count**, which T3 admits (every facade addition of S7 and S8 did the same), and without it no test can tell "two of the three ids named live unread rows" from "the call did nothing". `setNotificationPort` is §13.2's own. **`setMediaPort` is not added**: §13.2 does not list it, the port has no implementation before Phase 11, and SWEEP takes it as a constructor argument, so the wiring supplies `MediaPort.NONE` today and Phase 11's store later — a facade setter would be a promise nothing keeps. `ExpiryWake` is **one** port for both of §10.7's platform triggers (the in-process timer and the alarm) with null as the cancel, because a two-method port would let a caller schedule without ever cancelling, which is the state a rooted examiner reads an expiry minute out of (L7-D7). `DmCheckpoint` is a port rather than a direct call because `SyncDatabase.sql` is `internal` to `:sync` — deliberately, since every DM write goes through a `SyncTransaction` — and the pragma must run **outside** one |
| 7 | §10.7 R-K1, §13.7 "Wiring" | **R-K1 is `:storage`'s, in `GhostDatabase.finishOpen`**, between the WAL pragma and `verifyIntegrity`, so no read of the connection precedes it. It returns whether the log was truncated rather than raising: a busy log is another connection's, not a failed open. It is the other end of the in-memory `checkpoint_due` of §10.7 — that flag dies with the process, so without an open checkpoint a device that swept and was then killed would keep the freed pages in `-wal` until its next deletion, which is the T25 physical bound broken for an unbounded time |
| 8 | §10.7 point 4, §22.10 item 3 | **The expired-row lint is split in two, and its own stated rule is made true.** Its KDoc has always said "writes are not read paths and are left alone", and the scanner kept that promise only for writes with no subquery — but SWEEP's batched delete has to be `DELETE … WHERE message_id IN (SELECT …)`, because SQLite's `DELETE` takes no `LIMIT` unless it was built with `SQLITE_ENABLE_UPDATE_DELETE_LIMIT` and Android's is not. The skip is now anchored on the statement's first keyword. SWEEP's own `SELECT` — the one that finds the batch and hands the same ids to the media port — is registered in a second registry, `DmSweepQueries`, judged by the **mirror** of the read contract: it must name every expired row of the fixture world and **nothing at all** in the world after the sweep. A UI read moved there to escape the read contract names live rows and fails; a boundary the wrong way round misses a fixture row and fails the completeness half |
| 9 | §16.1 gate G-DM2 | **A Kotlin safe call was invisible to the gate, and `Outbox.cancel` is exactly the call that would have used it.** The receiver check matched `outbox.cancel(` and not `outbox?.cancel(`, so the whole of mutant **ME-21** could be written in `Sweeper.kt` — a file on `REMOVE_ONLY` and not on `ALLOWED_FILES`, which is supposed to refuse it — and pass. Found while checking that the comment claiming the gate enforces ME-21 was true; it was not. `?` is now admitted everywhere `.` is, including in the continuation fold, and the `negative-dm-enqueue-evasion` fixture gains a fourth shape (its expected hit count moves from 3 to 4). `ALLOWED_FILES` and `REMOVE_ONLY` are unchanged: `Sweeper.kt` was put on `REMOVE_ONLY` by S6, before the file existed, and this is the slice that makes that entry do work. **This item's own claim was still too wide and item 16 corrects it**: the safe call was one of two holes, and the other one — a port injected under a name of the author's choosing — was the shape `Sweeper.kt` itself uses |
| 10 | §21.9 rule 1, §10.7 trigger 1 | **Deferred: the actor timer's minimum does not include `t_pub` or the next week boundary.** §21.9 rule 1 sets it to "min(next `delete_at_minute`, `t_pub`, next `due_minute`, next week boundary, next EST timer)". `Sweeper.nextWakeMinute` computes the minimum over every **row-driven** deadline the schema holds — `dm_message.delete_at_minute`, `dm_held.hold_until_minute`, `dm_scheduled.due_minute`, `olm_session.prov_erase_minute` and `dm_contact.fetch_listen_until_minute` — and over nothing else. `t_pub(w)` is not a row: it is `start(w) + HMAC(S_0, …) mod 7 d`, derived inside `OwnerSteps` from the own card, and the actor deliberately holds the owner pass as a `() -> Unit` rather than holding `OwnerSteps` (gate G-DM2's third rule, §22.11). **What the code does instead:** OWNER-WEEK keeps the three triggers §21.9 rule 1 already gives it — a database open, a relay session start, and any actor timer some *other* deadline caused — so a live device publishes within a pass of `t_pub`, and a device with no DM rows at all publishes at its next session. **What the slice that adds it must add:** a `nextOwnerWake: () -> Long?` beside `ownerMaintenance`, bound to a new `OwnerSteps.publicationDueMinute()`, with the week boundary folded into the same minimum; the natural owner is S12, which writes the wiring that would supply it |
| 11 | §13.7 "Wiring", §13.1 `…messaging.android` | **Deferred to S12: the receiver's manifest entry, and everything that binds the wakes to the process.** `ExpiryAlarm`, `ExpiryAlarmReceiver`, `ExpiryTimer` and `ExpiryTimer.both` are all here, and `ExpiryAlarmSpecTest` pins every clause of §21.10 point 7 that can be checked without a device — which became true only with item 15(g); as this slice first wrote it, the test bound the trigger-time clause and described the other four. The gate that is supposed to guard the manifest entry is **also** not armed, and item 16 says what S12 owes there. What is **not** here is `<receiver android:name="…ExpiryAlarmReceiver" android:exported="false" />`, and it cannot be: a library manifest cannot declare a receiver without an `<application>` element, and T15's `manifest-lint.sh` requires any manifest that has one to carry `allowBackup`, `dataExtractionRules`, `fullBackupContent` and `networkSecurityConfig` — the app's facts, not a library's. Until S12 declares it, the alarm schedules against a component the system cannot resolve and `ExpiryAlarmReceiver.onAlarm` is never set, so **on a device the alarm is inert**. The same wiring owns three things beside it, and none is in this branch either: the `onTimeTrigger` at the first database open (§13.7's "runs a sweep, R-K1 and the resumable ACTIVATE check at the first database open" — R-K1 itself *is* here, in `:storage`), the foreground-session-start trigger, and the `wakeIn(null)` at **wipe** that §10.7 asks for ("cancelled when nothing is pending and at wipe"). The mechanism for the last is `ExpiryWake`'s null and `ExpiryTimer.close()`; what is missing is the call site, which sits beside `ctxClose` in the wipe path. The consumer tail is the one trigger of the four that this branch really drives, because `DmActor.consumerTail` is already the seam `:sync` calls. `dm-emulator.yml` (S12) is where any of this is exercised on a device |
| 12 | §15.5 EP, §15.1 | **Deferred to S10 and S11: the EP suite as §15.5 enumerates it.** What this slice tests is the behaviour, one case per rule: `EphemeralTest` for §10.3's bounds through the facade (sender; unread with an honest, a short-lying and a long-lying relay expiry; displayed; the stale `markDisplayed`; ME-19; ME-21 in both halves, the cancel that does not happen and the outcome that still lands; the four R-T9 cases, each driven by two devices acting rather than by a column written by hand (item 15); R-T3; the wake, including under a clock stepped back a day; and the physical scan), `SweepTest` for the cascade table by table (the generation-and-hold ordering, the retired session, `prov_env`, the request, the peer PQ key, the orphan namespace, R-T7's clock step, ME-10's busy-reader retry and the checkpoint that fails outright), and the two port contracts. What is **not** here is the **cross product** §10.9 asks for — every timer × every path — nor the request and pending-send paths end to end, nor the held replay at 1 h and 30 d, nor §10.9 point 5's capture assertions, which need a `ModelRelay` rather than this world's envelope path. Those are S10's, and NI-DM4's "captures for a 5-minute and a 1-hour timer are identical" is S11's twin. One EP case of §21.10 point 2 is **not constructible in this world at all** and S10 owes the world before it owes the test: the HIGH-mode message whose `not_before_minute` falls after its own expiry. Both harnesses hard-wire `PrivacyMode.STANDARD` and every enqueue uses `SendDelay.DEFAULT`, so `not_before` is never in the future and the shape cannot arise |
| 13 | §22.12 items 12 and 14, §22.13 items 9 and 12 | **Unchanged deferrals.** The off-schedule bundle rank is still 1 and not `1 + max(rank)`; the hint benchmark of Q37 and V-13 is still not run; a FAILED reconnect or revocation control is still not rescheduled (`dm_scheduled` has no attempt column and v5 is closed); `bundleReseal` is still not on the closed list and `dm_self.bundle_published_week` still has no reader. None of the four is ephemeral work and none was touched. Recorded here so the count of open items is not lost between slices |
